Lemmas~1.5 and~1.6 are two applications of one ``exhaustion principle'' (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:exhaustion}), and Eqs.~(1.88) and~(1.94) two applications of one decoupling statement for cells (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:seq-decoupling}).
The Aldous--Hoover theorem for \emph{separately} exchangeable arrays (Theorem~1.4) and the bridge from Theorem~1.5 to the overlap array are formalized below; the Dovbysh--Sudakov theorem (Theorem~1.7) is formalized in Section~\ref{sec:sg:dovbysh-sudakov}, and Lemma~1.7 (measurable reconstruction of the random measure) is formalized in Section~\ref{sec:sg:reconstruction}.
Deviations are listed in Remark~\ref{rem:sg:aldous-hoover-deviation}.

\begin{lemma}[$L^2$ projection equality for nested conditional expectations]\label{lem:sg:l2proj}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.integral_mul_condExp_of_stronglyMeasurable,
        MeasureTheory.integral_condExp_mul_condExp_of_le,
        MeasureTheory.integral_sq_condExp_sub_condExp_of_le,
        MeasureTheory.integral_sq_condExp_le_of_le,
        MeasureTheory.condExp_ae_eq_of_integral_sq_condExp_eq,
        MeasureTheory.condExp_ae_eq_of_le_of_le}
  \leanok
Let $\mu$ be a finite measure on $(\Omega,\mathscr B)$, let $\mathscr M_1\subseteq\mathscr M_2\subseteq\mathscr B$ be $\sigma$-algebras and $f\in L^2(\mu)$. Write $f_i=\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_i]$. Then
$\mathbb E[f_1f_2]=\mathbb E[f_1^2]$ and
\[\mathbb E\big[(f_2-f_1)^2\big]=\mathbb E[f_2^2]-\mathbb E[f_1^2].\]
In particular $\mathbb E[f_1^2]\le\mathbb E[f_2^2]$, and if $\mathbb E[f_1^2]=\mathbb E[f_2^2]$ then $f_1=f_2$ almost surely.
Moreover (sandwich): if $\mathscr M_1\subseteq\mathscr M_2\subseteq\mathscr M_3\subseteq\mathscr B$ and $\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_1]=\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_3]$ almost surely (for any integrable $f$), then
$\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_2]=\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_3]$ almost surely.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Since $\mathscr M_1\subseteq\mathscr M_2$, the tower property gives $\mathbb E[f_2\mid\mathscr M_1]=f_1$. As $f_1$ is $\mathscr M_1$-measurable and $f_1,f_2\in L^2$, pulling $f_1$ out and using
$\mathbb E\big[\mathbb E[\,\cdot\mid\mathscr M_1]\big]=\mathbb E[\,\cdot\,]$ yields $\mathbb E[f_1f_2]=\mathbb E[f_1\,\mathbb E[f_2\mid\mathscr M_1]]=\mathbb E[f_1^2]$. Expanding $(f_2-f_1)^2$ gives the displayed identity. If the norms are
equal, the non-negative function $(f_2-f_1)^2$ has integral zero, hence vanishes almost surely.
For the sandwich, $\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_2]=\mathbb E\big[\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_3]\mid\mathscr M_2\big]=\mathbb E\big[\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_1]\mid\mathscr M_2\big]=\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_1]$, because $\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_1]$ is $\mathscr M_2$-measurable.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Coding Lemma, Lemma~1.4 of the book]\label{lem:sg:coding}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.Kernel.exists_measurable_map_compProd_eq,
        ProbabilityTheory.exists_coding_function,
        ProbabilityTheory.identDistrib_coding,
        ProbabilityTheory.exists_coding_identDistrib}
  \leanok
Let $(X,Y)$ be a random pair on a finite measure space $(\Omega,P)$ with $X$ taking values in a measurable space $\Omega_1$ and $Y$ in a standard Borel space $\Omega_2$ (both a.e.\ measurable). Then there is
a measurable $f:\Omega_1\times[0,1]\to\Omega_2$ such that the image of $\mathrm{law}(X)\otimes\mathrm{Leb}$ under $(x,u)\mapsto(x,f(x,u))$ is the law of $(X,Y)$. Consequently, on any probability space
carrying $X'$ with the law of $X$ and a uniform $U$ independent of $X'$ we have $(X,Y)\overset d=(X',f(X',U))$; for instance on $\Omega\times[0,1]$ with the product measure, $(X,Y)\overset d=(X,f(X,u))$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Disintegrate the law of $(X,Y)$ as $\mathrm{law}(X)\otimes\kappa$ for a Markov kernel $\kappa$ from $\Omega_1$ to $\Omega_2$ (the regular conditional distribution of $Y$ given $X$, which exists because $\Omega_2$ is standard Borel). A
Markov kernel into a standard Borel space is the image of the uniform law by a jointly measurable map: there is a measurable $f$ with $\mathrm{Leb}\circ f(x,\cdot)^{-1}=\kappa(x,\cdot)$ for all $x$ (Kallenberg, Lemma~4.22, in Mathlib). Evaluating both
sides on a measurable set $S\subseteq\Omega_1\times\Omega_2$ by Fubini gives $\int\mathrm{Leb}\{u:(x,f(x,u))\in S\}\,\mathrm{law}(X)(dx)=\int\kappa(x,S_x)\,\mathrm{law}(X)(dx)$. The transport statement holds because the joint law of $(X',U)$ is the product
$\mathrm{law}(X)\otimes\mathrm{Leb}$ by independence.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sequential conditioning gives a product formula, cf.\ Eqs.~(1.88) and~(1.94)]\label{thm:sg:seq-decoupling}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.condExp_mul_eq_mul_condExp_of_condExp_eq,
        MeasureTheory.condExp_prod_eq_prod_condExp,
        MeasureTheory.condExp_prod_comp_eq_prod_condExp,
        MeasureTheory.condExp_finset_prod_comp_eq_prod_condExp}
  \leanok
Let $\mu$ be a finite measure on $(\Omega,\mathscr B)$, $\mathscr M\subseteq\mathscr B$ a $\sigma$-algebra, and $Y_1,\dots,Y_n$ measurable random variables. Let $f_1,\dots,f_n$ be bounded measurable real functions. Suppose that
for each $k$, writing $\mathscr G_k=\mathscr M\vee\sigma(Y_j:j<k)$,
\[\mathbb E[f_k(Y_k)\mid\mathscr G_k]=\mathbb E[f_k(Y_k)\mid\mathscr M]\quad\text{a.s.}\]
Then $\mathbb E\big[\prod_{k\le n}f_k(Y_k)\mid\mathscr M\big]=\prod_{k\le n}\mathbb E[f_k(Y_k)\mid\mathscr M]$ almost surely. More generally the same holds for bounded measurable $Z_k$ and any
$\sigma$-algebras $\mathscr M\subseteq\mathscr G_k\subseteq\mathscr B$ for which $Z_j$ is $\mathscr G_k$-measurable whenever $j<k$.

\emph{Cell version.} Let $(s_c)_{c\in T}$ be measurable random variables indexed by a set $T$ of ``cells'', write $\mathscr F_K=\sigma(s_c:c\in K)$ for $K\subseteq T$, let $K\subseteq T$, let $t$ be a finite index set, $l:t\to T\setminus K$ injective and $f_i$ ($i\in t$) bounded measurable.
Suppose that for every $i\in t$ and every $K'\supseteq K$ with $l(i)\notin K'$ we have $\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_{K'}]=\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_K]$ almost surely. Then
$\mathbb E\big[\prod_{i\in t}f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_K\big]=\prod_{i\in t}\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_K]$ almost surely.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Induction on $n$. Put $A=\prod_{j<n}Z_j$, which is bounded and $\mathscr G_n$-measurable. By the tower property, the pull-out property, the hypothesis and the pull-out property again,
\[\mathbb E[AZ_n\mid\mathscr M]=\mathbb E\big[\mathbb E[AZ_n\mid\mathscr G_n]\mid\mathscr M\big]=\mathbb E\big[A\,\mathbb E[Z_n\mid\mathscr G_n]\mid\mathscr M\big]=\mathbb E\big[A\,\mathbb E[Z_n\mid\mathscr M]\mid\mathscr M\big]=\mathbb E[A\mid\mathscr M]\,\mathbb E[Z_n\mid\mathscr M],\]
and the induction hypothesis applied to $Z_1,\dots,Z_{n-1}$ identifies $\mathbb E[A\mid\mathscr M]$.
For the cell version enumerate $t=\{i_1,\dots,i_n\}$, put $Y_k=s_{l(i_k)}$ and $\mathscr G_k=\mathscr F_K\vee\sigma(Y_j:j<k)=\mathscr F_{K\cup\{l(i_j):j<k\}}$; the set $K\cup\{l(i_j):j<k\}$ contains $K$ and not $l(i_k)$, so the hypothesis gives the one of the sequential statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional expectations only depend on the joint law]\label{lem:sg:condexp-law}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.condDistrib_eq_of_map_eq,
        ProbabilityTheory.integral_sq_condExp_comp_eq_integral_condDistrib,
        ProbabilityTheory.integral_sq_condExp_comp_eq_of_map_eq,
        ProbabilityTheory.condExp_comp_ae_eq_of_map_eq}
  \leanok
Let $P$ be a finite measure on $\Omega$, $B:\Omega\to\beta$ measurable, $A:\Omega\to E$ a.e.\ measurable with $E$ a nonempty standard Borel space, and $F:E\to\mathbb R$ bounded and measurable. Then
$\mathbb E[F(A)\mid\sigma(B)]=\psi(B)$ almost surely, where $\psi(b)=\int F\,d\kappa(b,\cdot)$ and $\kappa$ is the regular conditional distribution of $A$ given $B$; and $\kappa$ is determined by the law of $(B,A)$.
Consequently:
(a) if $(B,A)\overset d=(B,A')$ under $P$, then $\mathbb E[F(A)\mid\sigma(B)]=\mathbb E[F(A')\mid\sigma(B)]$ almost surely;
(b) if $(B,A)$ under $P$ has the same law as $(B',A')$ under a (possibly different) finite measure $P'$, then
$\int\big(\mathbb E_P[F(A)\mid\sigma(B)]\big)^2\,dP=\int\big(\mathbb E_{P'}[F(A')\mid\sigma(B')]\big)^2\,dP'$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The formula $\mathbb E[F(A)\mid\sigma(B)]=\int F\,d\kappa(B,\cdot)$ is Mathlib's description of conditional expectations through \texttt{condDistrib}, and \texttt{condDistrib} is by definition the disintegration of the law of $(B,A)$,
so it depends on $(B,A)$ only through that law. This gives (a). For (b), the second moment of $\psi(B)$ is $\int\psi^2\,d(\mathrm{law}\,B)$, and both the law of $B$ (the first marginal of the law of $(B,A)$) and $\psi$ are the same on both sides.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Exhaustion principle: the common core of Lemmas~1.5 and~1.6]\label{lem:sg:exhaustion}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.condExp_comp_ae_eq_of_exhaustion}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:sg:l2proj, lem:sg:condexp-law, lem:sg:levy}
Let $P$ be a finite measure, $(s_c)_{c\in T}$ measurable random variables with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$, $t\in T$ and $F:E\to\mathbb R$ bounded and measurable. Let $A_n,A'_n:\kappa_n\to T$ ($n\in\mathbb N$) be two sequences of
families of cells that are increasing (the range of $A_n$ is contained in the range of $A_{n+1}$, and likewise for $A'_n$), with $\bigcup_n\operatorname{ran}A_n\subseteq\bigcup_n\operatorname{ran}A'_n$. Write $\mathscr F_C=\sigma(s_c:c\in C)$.
Suppose that for every $n$ the random element $\big((s_{A_n(k)})_{k\in\kappa_n},s_t\big)$ has the same law as $\big((s_{A'_n(k)})_{k\in\kappa_n},s_t\big)$. Then
\[\mathbb E\big[F(s_t)\mid\mathscr F_{\bigcup_n\operatorname{ran}A_n}\big]=\mathbb E\big[F(s_t)\mid\mathscr F_{\bigcup_n\operatorname{ran}A'_n}\big]\quad\text{almost surely.}\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:condexp-law}(b) the $L^2$ norms of $\mathbb E[F(s_t)\mid\sigma(s_{A_n(k)}:k)]$ and $\mathbb E[F(s_t)\mid\sigma(s_{A'_n(k)}:k)]$ agree for each $n$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:levy} they converge, as $n\to\infty$, to the $L^2$ norms of the conditional
expectations given the two unions, which are therefore equal. The first $\sigma$-algebra is contained in the second, so Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:l2proj} gives equality almost surely.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The conditional law given an exchangeable pair is symmetric]\label{lem:sg:cond-swap}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.condDistrib_swap_ae_eq}
  \leanok
Let $P$ be a finite measure, $A,B:\Omega\to\alpha$ and $Y:\Omega\to E$ measurable, $E$ a nonempty standard Borel space, and suppose $\big((B,A),Y\big)\overset d=\big((A,B),Y\big)$. Let $\nu$ be the regular conditional distribution of $Y$ given $(A,B)$. Then
$\nu(b,a)=\nu(a,b)$ for $\mathrm{law}(A,B)$-almost every $(a,b)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Write $\mu=\mathrm{law}(A,B)$, so the law of $((A,B),Y)$ is $\mu\otimes\nu$. The map $T((a,b),y)=((b,a),y)$ leaves $\mu\otimes\nu$ invariant by hypothesis, and $\mu$ is invariant under $(a,b)\mapsto(b,a)$ (first marginal). The image of $\mu\otimes(\nu\circ\mathrm{swap})$ under $T$ is
$(\mu\circ\mathrm{swap}^{-1})\otimes\nu=\mu\otimes\nu$; applying $T$ again (an involution) gives $\mu\otimes(\nu\circ\mathrm{swap})=\mu\otimes\nu$. Two kernels with the same composition product with $\mu$ agree $\mu$-almost everywhere (the target is standard Borel).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Convergence of $L^2$ norms along a filtration (L\'evy's upward theorem)]\label{lem:sg:levy}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.tendsto_integral_sq_condExp}
  \leanok
Let $\mu$ be a finite measure, $\mathscr G_1\subseteq\mathscr G_2\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathscr B$ $\sigma$-algebras, $\mathscr G_\infty=\bigvee_n\mathscr G_n$, and $f$ a function with $|f|\le C$. Then
$\int\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr G_n]^2\,d\mu\to\int\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr G_\infty]^2\,d\mu$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
By L\'evy's upward theorem (the martingale convergence theorem in Mathlib), $\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr G_n]\to\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr G_\infty]$ almost everywhere. Conditional expectations of a function bounded by $C$ are bounded by $C$,
so the squares are dominated by $C^2$, which is integrable for a finite measure; apply dominated convergence.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditionally independent families are mixtures of product measures]\label{lem:sg:cond-iid}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.map_prodMk_eq_compProd_infinitePi,
        ProbabilityTheory.map_eq_bind_infinitePi_condDistrib,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.measurable_pi_family,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.measurable_infinitePi,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.map_bind,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.measurable_pi_curry,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.measurable_map_curry,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.map_prod_infinitePi_eq_compProd,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.map_prod_infinitePi_eq_bind}
  \leanok
Let $(\Omega,P)$ be a probability space, $X:\Omega\to\beta$ and $Y_i:\Omega\to E$ ($i\in\iota$, $\iota$ any index set) measurable, and $\kappa_i$ Markov kernels from $\beta$ to $E$. Assume that for all bounded measurable $f,f_i:E\to\mathbb R$ and all finite $t\subseteq\iota$,
\[\mathbb E[f(Y_i)\mid\sigma(X)]=\int f\,d\kappa_i(X,\cdot),\qquad
\mathbb E\Big[\prod_{i\in t}f_i(Y_i)\,\Big|\,\sigma(X)\Big]=\prod_{i\in t}\mathbb E[f_i(Y_i)\mid\sigma(X)]\quad\text{a.s.}\]
Then (a) the law of $(X,(Y_i)_{i\in\iota})$ on $\beta\times E^{\iota}$ is $\mathrm{law}(X)\otimes K$, where $K(x)=\bigotimes_i\kappa_i(x,\cdot)$: given $X$, the $Y_i$ are independent with laws $\kappa_i(X,\cdot)$.
(b) If $E$ is standard Borel, $\iota=\mathbb N$, and $\mathbb E[f(Y_i)\mid\sigma(X)]=\mathbb E[f(Y_0)\mid\sigma(X)]$ for all $i$, then the law of $(Y_i)_{i\in\mathbb N}$ is
$\int\kappa(x,\cdot)^{\otimes\mathbb N}\,\mathrm{law}(X)(dx)$, a mixture of i.i.d.\ sequences, where $\kappa$ is the regular conditional distribution of $Y_0$ given $X$.
(c) Conversely, if $a\sim\mu$ and $(c_i)_{i\in\iota}$ are i.i.d.\ $\nu$ and independent of $a$, and $g_i$ are measurable, then $\big(a,(g_i(a,c_i))_i\big)$ has the law $\mu\otimes\tilde K$ with $\tilde K(a)=\bigotimes_i(\nu\circ g_i(a,\cdot)^{-1})$; for $g_i=g$ the law of
$(g(a,c_i))_{i}$ is $\int(\nu\circ g(a,\cdot)^{-1})^{\otimes\iota}\,\mu(da)$. Mixtures are compatible with pushforwards, and a measurable family of probability measures has a measurable infinite product (the auxiliary statements \texttt{measurable\_infinitePi}, \texttt{map\_bind}).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
(a) Two probability measures on $\beta\times E^{\iota}$ agree if they agree on the rectangles $A\times\prod_{i\in t}u_i\times E^{\iota\setminus t}$ ($A$ measurable, $t$ finite, $u_i$ measurable), which form a $\pi$-system generating the product $\sigma$-algebra.
The mass the law of $(X,Y)$ gives to such a rectangle is $\mathbb E\big[\mathbf 1_A(X)\prod_{i\in t}\mathbf 1_{u_i}(Y_i)\big]$. Since $\{X\in A\}\in\sigma(X)$ we may replace the product by its conditional expectation given $\sigma(X)$ (tower property); by the product formula and the
description of the marginals this is $\mathbb E\big[\mathbf 1_A(X)\prod_{i\in t}\kappa_i(X,u_i)\big]=\int_A\prod_{i\in t}\kappa_i(x,u_i)\,\mathrm{law}(X)(dx)$, which is the mass $\mathrm{law}(X)\otimes K$ gives to the rectangle (Mathlib: \texttt{infinitePi\_pi}).
(b) Take $\kappa_i=\kappa$ for all $i$ (the description of the marginals is Mathlib's formula for conditional expectations through \texttt{condDistrib}) and project to the second factor. (c) is Fubini and the formula for the image of an infinite product under coordinatewise maps.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Exchangeable sequences.}
A sequence $(s_l)_{l\in\mathbb N}$ of random variables with values in a measurable space $E$ is \emph{exchangeable} if $(s_{e(l)})_{l}\overset d=(s_l)_{l}$ for every bijection $e:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$ (equality of laws on $E^{\mathbb N}$).
This is the hypothesis \texttt{hexch} of all statements below; no structure or definition is introduced. Panchenko's Eq.~(1.81) asks for this only for permutations moving finitely many indices. The two are equivalent
(the restriction of a bijection of $\mathbb N$ to finitely many coordinates is the restriction of a finite permutation), but this equivalence is \emph{not} formalized; every theorem below is proved under the invariance for all bijections.
This is the form produced by mixtures of i.i.d.\ sequences (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:mixture}) and used for the overlap array in Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:annealed-gram}.

\begin{lemma}[Invariance along injections, Eq.~(1.84)]\label{lem:sg:subseq}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_equiv_comp_eq,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_comp_of_exchangeable,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_subsequence_of_exchangeable}
  \leanok
Let $(s_l)$ be exchangeable. (a) If $a,b:\iota\to\mathbb N$ are injective and $\iota$ is finite, then $(s_{a(i)})_{i\in\iota}\overset d=(s_{b(i)})_{i\in\iota}$. (b) For every injective $\theta:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$,
$(s_{\theta(l)})_{l\in\mathbb N}\overset d=(s_l)_{l\in\mathbb N}$; in particular this holds for the subsequence along any infinite $I\subseteq\mathbb N$ enumerated increasingly.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
(a) Two injections of a finite set into $\mathbb N$ differ by a permutation of $\mathbb N$: extend the bijection $a(\iota)\to b(\iota)$ to a permutation $e$ (permutations of the finite set $a(\iota)\cup b(\iota)$ extended by the identity),
so $b=e\circ a$ and the claim follows by applying the exchangeability under $e$ and then restricting to the coordinates $a(\iota)$. (b) The laws of the two sequences on $E^{\mathbb N}$ agree if all their finite-dimensional marginals
agree (Mathlib: \texttt{identDistrib\_iff\_forall\_finset\_identDistrib}), and for a finite set $J$ of coordinates this is (a) with $a=\theta|_J$ and $b=\mathrm{id}_J$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma~1.5 of the book]\label{lem:sg:lemma15}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.condExp_comp_ae_eq_condExp_compl}
  \leanok
Let $(s_l)_{l\in\mathbb N}$ be exchangeable with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$, let $\mathscr F_J=\sigma(s_l:l\in J)$ for $J\subseteq\mathbb N$, and let $f:E\to\mathbb R$ be bounded and measurable.
If $J\subseteq\mathbb N$ is infinite and $j\notin J$, then $\mathbb E[f(s_j)\mid\mathscr F_J]=\mathbb E[f(s_j)\mid\mathscr F_{\mathbb N\setminus\{j\}}]$ almost surely.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:exhaustion, lem:sg:subseq}
Enumerate $J=\{u_1,u_2,\dots\}$ and $\mathbb N\setminus\{j\}=\{u'_1,u'_2,\dots\}$ and exhaust both sets by their first $n$ elements. For each $n$ the injections $(j,u_1,\dots,u_n)$ and $(j,u'_1,\dots,u'_n)$ of $\{0,\dots,n\}$ into $\mathbb N$ give
$(s_{u_1},\dots,s_{u_n},s_j)\overset d=(s_{u'_1},\dots,s_{u'_n},s_j)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subseq}(a), and $J\subseteq\mathbb N\setminus\{j\}$; apply Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:exhaustion}. (The $L^2$ norms of the conditional expectations given infinite sub-families of exchangeable sequences
therefore do not depend on the sub-family, which is the argument of the book.)
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional independence of the coordinates, Eq.~(1.88)]\label{thm:sg:cond-indep}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.condExp_comp_ae_eq_of_exchangeable,
        SpinGlasses.condExp_prod_comp_eq_prod_condExp_of_exchangeable}
  \leanok
Let $(s_l)$ be exchangeable with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$, $K\subseteq\mathbb N$ infinite, $\mathscr F_K=\sigma(s_k:k\in K)$. (a) If $i,i'\notin K$ and $f$ is bounded measurable, then
$\mathbb E[f(s_i)\mid\mathscr F_K]=\mathbb E[f(s_{i'})\mid\mathscr F_K]$ almost surely. (b) If $t$ is a finite set, $l:t\to\mathbb N\setminus K$ is injective and $f_i$ ($i\in t$) are bounded measurable, then
\[\mathbb E\Big[\prod_{i\in t}f_i(s_{l(i)})\,\Big|\,\mathscr F_K\Big]=\prod_{i\in t}\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_K]\quad\text{almost surely.}\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:seq-decoupling, lem:sg:lemma15, lem:sg:condexp-law}
(a) The transposition of $i$ and $i'$ fixes $K$ pointwise, so $\big((s_k)_{k\in K},s_i\big)\overset d=\big((s_k)_{k\in K},s_{i'}\big)$ by exchangeability, and Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:condexp-law}(a) applies.
(b) Apply the cell version of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:seq-decoupling}. Its hypothesis, for $K\subseteq K'$ with $l(i)\notin K'$, is Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma15} applied twice, once with $J=K'$ and once with $J=K$ (both infinite, neither containing $l(i)$): both conditional expectations equal $\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_{\mathbb N\setminus\{l(i)\}}]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[de Finetti representation, Theorem~1.6 of the book, and its converse]\label{thm:sg:de-finetti}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_de_finetti_representation,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_deFinetti_comp_equiv}
  \leanok
Let $(s_l)_{l\in\mathbb N}$ be an exchangeable sequence of random variables with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$ (for example $E=\mathbb R$ or $E=[0,1]^{\mathbb N}$). Then there is a measurable $g:[0,1]^2\to E$
such that $(s_l)_{l\in\mathbb N}\overset d=(g(w,u_l))_{l\in\mathbb N}$ as random elements of $E^{\mathbb N}$, where $w,u_1,u_2,\dots$ are independent and uniform on $[0,1]$.
Conversely, for every measurable $g:[0,1]^2\to E$ the sequence $(g(w,u_l))_{l}$ is exchangeable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:cond-iid, thm:sg:cond-indep, lem:sg:subseq, lem:sg:mixture}
Let $X=(s_{2m})_{m\ge0}$ be the even coordinates and $Y_i=s_{2i+1}$ the odd ones; the set of even indices is infinite. By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:cond-indep} the $Y_i$ are conditionally independent and identically distributed given $\sigma(X)=\mathscr F_{\text{even}}$,
so by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-iid} the law of $(Y_i)_i$ is $\int\kappa(x,\cdot)^{\otimes\mathbb N}\,\mathrm{law}(X)(dx)$, where $\kappa$ is the conditional law of $Y_0$ given $X$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subseq}(b) with $\theta(l)=2l+1$,
the sequence $(Y_i)_i$ has the same law as $(s_l)_l$. Since $E^{\mathbb N}$ is standard Borel, $\mathrm{law}(X)$ is the image of the uniform law by a measurable $\varphi:[0,1]\to E^{\mathbb N}$, and
since $E$ is standard Borel the kernel $\kappa$ is the image of the uniform law by a jointly measurable $h:E^{\mathbb N}\times[0,1]\to E$, that is, $\kappa(x,\cdot)=\mathrm{Leb}\circ h(x,\cdot)^{-1}$ (both facts are in Mathlib). Put $g(w,u)=h(\varphi(w),u)$; the law of
$(g(w,u_l))_l$ is $\int(\mathrm{Leb}\circ g(w,\cdot)^{-1})^{\otimes\mathbb N}\,dw=\int\kappa(\varphi(w),\cdot)^{\otimes\mathbb N}\,dw$, which is the law above. The converse is Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-iid} (last sentence) together with
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:mixture}: a mixture of i.i.d.\ sequences is invariant under every permutation of the indices.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Weakly exchangeable arrays.}
A symmetric array is a family $(s_c)_{c\in\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)}$ indexed by unordered pairs $c=\{l,l'\}$ (diagonal pairs allowed), $s_{l,l'}=s_{\{l,l'\}}=s_{l',l}$; indexing by pairs makes the symmetry automatic. It is \emph{weakly exchangeable} if
$(s_{e(c)})_{c}\overset d=(s_c)_{c}$ for every bijection $e:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$, where $e$ acts on pairs by $e\{l,l'\}=\{e(l),e(l')\}$ (equality of laws on $E^{\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)}$). As for sequences this is a hypothesis \texttt{hexch}, not a structure; invariance under
finitely supported permutations only (the book's Eq.~(1.87)) is equivalent, but that equivalence is not formalized. The values lie in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$. This is the form satisfied by the overlap array of Section~\ref{sec:sg:asymptotic-gibbs}
(Eq.~(1.74) and the limit $\mu$), written there for ordered pairs.

\begin{lemma}[Invariance along injections, Eq.~(1.89)]\label{lem:sg:subarray}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.identDistrib_map_of_weakly_exchangeable}
  \leanok
Let $(s_c)$ be weakly exchangeable and $\theta:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$ injective. Then $(s_{\{\theta(l),\theta(l')\}})_{\{l,l'\}}\overset d=(s_{\{l,l'\}})_{\{l,l'\}}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
It suffices to compare finite-dimensional marginals (Mathlib: \texttt{identDistrib\_iff\_forall\_finset\_identDistrib}). For a finite set $J$ of pairs let $V$ be the finite set of their endpoints. Two injections of $V$ into $\mathbb N$ differ by a permutation of $\mathbb N$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subseq}(a)),
so there is a bijection $e$ with $e(\theta(v))=v$ on $V$; then $s_{e(\theta(c))}=s_c$ for $c\in J$, and weak exchangeability under $e$ applied to the coordinates $\theta(c)$, $c\in J$, gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma~1.6 of the book]\label{lem:sg:lemma16}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_enum_of_infinite,
        SpinGlasses.condExp_comp_ae_eq_condExp_compl_of_weakly_exchangeable}
  \leanok
Let $(s_c)$ be weakly exchangeable with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$, $V\subseteq\mathbb N$ infinite, $j\ne j'$ in $V$, $t=\{j,j'\}$, and $f:E\to\mathbb R$ bounded and measurable. Let $C$ be a set of pairs with $t\notin C$ that contains every pair
$c\ne t$ with both endpoints in $V$. Then, with $\mathscr F_C=\sigma(s_c:c\in C)$,
\[\mathbb E[f(s_t)\mid\mathscr F_C]=\mathbb E[f(s_t)\mid\mathscr F_{\{c:c\ne t\}}]\quad\text{almost surely.}\]
(The book takes $C$ to be exactly the set of pairs inside $V$ other than $t$.)
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:exhaustion, lem:sg:l2proj, lem:sg:subseq}
Enumerate $V=\{w_0=j,w_1=j',w_2,\dots\}$ and $\mathbb N=\{w'_0=j,w'_1=j',w'_2,\dots\}$ injectively (Lean: \texttt{exists\_enum\_of\_infinite}) and exhaust the pairs inside $V$ other than $t$ by the pairs among $w_0,\dots,w_{n+1}$ other than $t$,
and likewise for $\mathbb N$ with $w'$. For each $n$ a bijection $e$ of $\mathbb N$ with $e(w_i)=w'_i$ for $i\le n+1$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subseq}(a) for injections) fixes $j$ and $j'$, hence $t$, and maps the pairs among $w_0,\dots,w_{n+1}$ to those among $w'_0,\dots,w'_{n+1}$;
weak exchangeability under $e$ shows that the joint laws with $s_t$ agree. Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:exhaustion} gives the claim for $C$ equal to the set of pairs inside $V$ other than $t$ (and the right-hand side with all pairs $\ne t$). For a general $C$ between this set and
$\{c\ne t\}$ apply the sandwich of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:l2proj}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional independence of the entries, Eq.~(1.94)]\label{thm:sg:cond-indep-array}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.condExp_prod_comp_eq_prod_condExp_of_weakly_exchangeable}
  \leanok
Let $(s_c)$ be weakly exchangeable with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$, $K$ a set of pairs, $t$ a finite index set and $l:t\to\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)\setminus K$ injective. Suppose that every $l(i)$ is a pair $\{j,j'\}$, $j\ne j'$, for which there is an infinite
$V\subseteq\mathbb N$ containing $j,j'$ such that every pair $c\ne l(i)$ with both endpoints in $V$ lies in $K$. Then for bounded measurable $f_i$,
\[\mathbb E\Big[\prod_{i\in t}f_i(s_{l(i)})\,\Big|\,\mathscr F_K\Big]=\prod_{i\in t}\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_K]\quad\text{almost surely.}\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:seq-decoupling, lem:sg:lemma16}
This is the cell version of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:seq-decoupling}: for $K\subseteq K'$ with $l(i)\notin K'$, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma16} applied with $C=K'$ and with $C=K$ shows that both conditional expectations equal $\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_{\{c\ne l(i)\}}]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Aldous--Hoover representation of weakly exchangeable arrays, Theorem~1.5 of the book, and its converse]\label{thm:sg:aldous-hoover}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.identDistrib_vertexData_comp_equiv,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_vertexPair,
        SpinGlasses.condExp_vertexData_ae_eq_integral,
        SpinGlasses.map_vertexData_eq_compProd,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_reindex,
        SpinGlasses.range_two_mul,
        SpinGlasses.infinite_even,
        Equiv.sym2Congr,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.measurePreserving_comp_equiv_infinitePi,
        SpinGlasses.map_codedCells_eq_compProd,
        SpinGlasses.map_codedArray_eq_compProd,
        SpinGlasses.exists_aldousHoover_representation,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_aldousHoover_comp_equiv}
  \leanok
Let $(s_c)_{c\in\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)}$ be a weakly exchangeable symmetric array of random variables with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$ on a probability space. Then there are measurable maps $g:[0,1]^2\to E$ and $f:[0,1]^4\to E$, with $f(w,u,u',x)=f(w,u',u,x)$, such that
\[\big(s_{\{l,l'\}}\big)_{l,l'\in\mathbb N}\overset d=\Big(\mathbf 1_{l=l'}\,g(w,u_l)+\mathbf 1_{l\ne l'}\,f\big(w,u_l,u_{l'},x_{\{l,l'\}}\big)\Big)_{l,l'\in\mathbb N}\]
(as random elements of $E^{\mathbb N\times\mathbb N}$; the right-hand side is the value $g(w,u_l)$ for $l=l'$ and $f(\dots)$ for $l\ne l'$), where $w$, $(u_l)_{l\in\mathbb N}$ and $(x_c)_{c\in\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)}$ are independent and uniform on $[0,1]$.
Conversely, for all measurable $g$ and measurable $f$ symmetric in its two middle arguments, the array on the right is weakly exchangeable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:de-finetti, thm:sg:cond-indep-array, lem:sg:lemma16, lem:sg:subarray, lem:sg:cond-iid, lem:sg:cond-swap, lem:sg:coding, lem:sg:mixture}
We follow the book, with the odd vertices $2a+1$ in the role of the infinite set $I$ and the even vertices in the role of $I^c$. For $a\in\mathbb N$ let $S_a$ be the array on the vertices $\{2a+1\}\cup\{\text{even numbers}\}$ (reindexed by $\mathbb N$ through
$\kappa_a(0)=2a+1$, $\kappa_a(m+1)=2m$): the vertex array of Eq.~(1.91). Permuting the odd vertices and fixing the even ones shows that $(S_a)_a$ is an exchangeable sequence with values in the standard Borel space $E^{\operatorname{Sym}^2\mathbb N}$ (even jointly with the entries
$Z_{\{a,b\}}=s_{\{2a+1,2b+1\}}$, Lean: \texttt{identDistrib\_vertexData\_comp\_equiv}). By de Finetti's theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:de-finetti}) $(S_a)_a\overset d=(X(w,u_a))_a$ for a measurable $X$.
(1) \emph{Conditional independence.} Let $\mathscr F=\sigma(S_a:a)$. It is generated by all the pairs except the off-diagonal pairs of odd vertices. For $a\ne b$, the set of pairs inside $V=\{\text{even}\}\cup\{2a+1,2b+1\}$ other than $\{2a+1,2b+1\}$ is contained in the pairs
generating $\sigma(S_a,S_b)$, which is contained in the pairs generating $\mathscr F$, which does not contain $\{2a+1,2b+1\}$. Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma16} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:cond-indep-array} give: the entries $Z_c$ ($c$ off-diagonal) are conditionally independent given $\mathscr F$ and
$\mathbb E[h(Z_{\{a,b\}})\mid\mathscr F]=\mathbb E[h(Z_{\{a,b\}})\mid\sigma(S_a,S_b)]=\int h\,d\nu(S_a,S_b)$, with the \emph{same} kernel $\nu$ (the conditional law of $Z_{\{0,1\}}$ given $(S_0,S_1)$) for all pairs, because $(S_a,S_b,Z_{\{a,b\}})\overset d=(S_0,S_1,Z_{\{0,1\}})$
by exchangeability. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-iid}(a) the law of $\big((S_a)_a,(Z_c)_c\big)$ is $\mathrm{law}(S)\otimes K$, $K(x)=\bigotimes_{c=\{a,b\}}\nu(x_a,x_b)$ (with an arbitrary orientation of each pair).
(2) \emph{Coding.} Code $\nu$ by a uniform variable, $\nu(p,\cdot)=\mathrm{Leb}\circ H(p,\cdot)^{-1}$ (Mathlib, as in Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:coding}), and put $f(w,u,u',x)=H\big((X(w,u),X(w,u')),x\big)$ if $u\le u'$ and $H\big((X(w,u'),X(w,u)),x\big)$ otherwise, which is symmetric.
The swapped pair $(S_1,S_0,Z_{\{0,1\}})$ has the same law as $(S_0,S_1,Z_{\{0,1\}})$ by exchangeability, so $\nu$ is symmetric almost everywhere (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-swap}), and therefore the entries
$f(w,u_a,u_b,x_{\{a,b\}})$ also have, given $(w,(u_a))$, independent laws $\nu\big(X(w,u_a),X(w,u_b)\big)$ for $u_a\le u_b$ and $\nu(X(w,u_b),X(w,u_a))=\nu(X(w,u_a),X(w,u_b))$ for almost every $(w,u)$ otherwise.
Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-iid}(c) gives that the vertex arrays $(X(w,u_a))_a$ together with these entries have the law $\mathrm{law}(X(w,u_\cdot))\otimes K=\mathrm{law}(S)\otimes K$.
(3) \emph{Assembly.} The array on the odd vertices is the image of $\big((S_a),(Z_c)\big)$ under a fixed measurable map (the diagonal entry of $a$ is the entry $\{0,0\}$ of $S_a$, the off-diagonal ones are the $Z_c$), so it has the law of the coded array with $g(w,u)=X(w,u)_{\{0,0\}}$.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subarray} with $\theta(l)=2l+1$ the array on the odd vertices has the law of the whole array.
The converse: relabelling the vertices by a bijection $e$ turns $(u_l)$ into $(u_{e(l)})$ and $(x_c)$ into $(x_{e(c)})$, which has the same law because infinite i.i.d.\ families are invariant under permutations of the index set (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:mixture}); the symmetry of $f$ makes the value at the pair $\{l,l'\}$ independent of the order.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Curried arrays and Gram-type laws.}
The overlap array of Section~\ref{sec:sg:asymptotic-gibbs} is a \emph{curried} array $A=(A_{l,l'})_{l,l'}$ which is only almost surely symmetric, with a law $\Lambda$ on $\mathbb R^{\mathbb N\times\mathbb N}$ invariant under $A\mapsto(A_{e(l),e(l')})_{l,l'}$ for every bijection $e$ of $\mathbb N$. The next two statements transfer Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover} to this form.

\begin{theorem}[Theorem~1.5 for a curried array]\label{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-curried}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_aldousHoover_representation_of_curried}
  \leanok
Let $R=(R_{l,l'})_{l,l'\in\mathbb N}$ be a random array with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$ such that almost surely $R_{l,l'}=R_{l',l}$ for all $l,l'$, and $(R_{e(l),e(l')})_{l,l'}\overset d=(R_{l,l'})_{l,l'}$ for every bijection $e$ of $\mathbb N$. Then the conclusion of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover} holds for $(R_{l,l'})_{(l,l')\in\mathbb N\times\mathbb N}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:aldous-hoover}
Put $s_{\{a,b\}}=R_{\min(a,b),\max(a,b)}$; this is measurable for the pairs, and on symmetric arrays $s_{\{a,b\}}=R_{a,b}$, so $s$ equals $R$ almost surely as an array on $\mathbb N\times\mathbb N$. For a bijection $e$ both $(s_{e(c)})_c$ and the image of $(R_{e(l),e(l')})$ under the same measurable map agree almost surely with $(R_{e(a),e(b)})_{\{a,b\}}$ (symmetry of $R$), so $s$ is weakly exchangeable by the hypothesis. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover} and transfer along the almost sure equality.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Aldous--Hoover representation of Gram-type laws]\label{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-gram}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_aldousHoover_representation_of_gram,
        SpinGlasses.exists_aldousHoover_representation_of_gram_set}
  \leanok
Let $\Lambda$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb R^{\mathbb N\times\mathbb N}$ which is concentrated on symmetric arrays with unit diagonal ($A_{l,l'}=A_{l',l}$ and $A_{l,l}=1$) and invariant under $A\mapsto(A_{e(l),e(l')})_{l,l'}$ for every bijection $e$ of $\mathbb N$. Then there is a measurable $f:[0,1]^4\to\mathbb R$, symmetric in its two middle arguments, such that $\Lambda$ is the law of
\[\Big(\mathbf 1_{l=l'}+\mathbf 1_{l\ne l'}\,f\big(w,u_l,u_{l'},x_{\{l,l'\}}\big)\Big)_{l,l'\in\mathbb N},\]
with $w$, $(u_l)$, $(x_c)_{c\in\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)}$ independent and uniform on $[0,1]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-curried}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-curried} to the identity array on $(\mathbb R^{\mathbb N\times\mathbb N},\Lambda)$ and let $g,f$ be the resulting maps. The set of arrays in $\mathbb R^{\mathbb N\times\mathbb N}$ with unit diagonal is measurable and has full measure under $\Lambda$, hence also under the law of the right-hand side, that is, $g(w,u_l)=1$ for all $l$ almost surely. Replacing $g$ by $1$ changes the array only on a null set.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-gram} applies to the asymptotic overlap law $\mu$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:asymptotic-overlap}: that theorem gives a probability measure concentrated on the closed set of symmetric, unit diagonal, positive semidefinite arrays with entries in $[-1,1]$ which is exchangeable. The Lean declaration \texttt{exists\_aldousHoover\_representation\_of\_gram\_set} takes exactly this form of hypothesis (the Gram set has measure one) and so applies to any limit produced by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:asymptotic-overlap}, without restating its hypotheses. Positive semidefiniteness and the bound are not used here; that they force $f$ not to depend on the cell noise $x_{\{l,l'\}}$ is the Dovbysh--Sudakov theorem, formalized in Section~\ref{sec:sg:dovbysh-sudakov}.

\paragraph{Separately exchangeable arrays.}
An array $(s_{l,l'})_{l,l'\in\mathbb N}$ with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$ is \emph{separately exchangeable} if $(s_{e_1(l),e_2(l')})_{l,l'}\overset d=(s_{l,l'})_{l,l'}$ for every pair of bijections $e_1,e_2$ of $\mathbb N$ (Eq.~(1.76); as above, a hypothesis \texttt{hexch} on all bijections, not only finitely supported ones).

\begin{lemma}[Restrictions of an i.i.d.\ family to disjoint index sets are independent]\label{lem:sg:iid-split}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.Measure.indepFun_restrict_infinitePi_of_disjoint,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.map_infinitePi_pair_of_disjoint,
        MeasureTheory.Measure.map_prod_infinitePi_pair_of_disjoint}
  \leanok
Let $(c_i)_{i\in\iota}$ be i.i.d.\ with law $\nu$, $f:\alpha\to\iota$ and $g:\beta\to\iota$ injective with disjoint ranges, and $a$ a random element independent of $(c_i)$. Then $a$, $(c_{f(\cdot)})$ and $(c_{g(\cdot)})$ are independent, the last two being i.i.d.\ families with law $\nu$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The $\sigma$-algebras generated by the coordinates in two disjoint sets of indices are independent (Mathlib: \texttt{indep\_iSup\_of\_disjoint}); the sub-family along an injection is i.i.d.\ (Mathlib: \texttt{map\_infinitePi\_infinitePi\_of\_inj}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Invariance along injections, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subarray} for separately exchangeable arrays]\label{lem:sg:subarray-sep}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.identDistrib_map_of_separately_exchangeable}
  \leanok
For injections $\theta_1,\theta_2:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$, $(s_{\theta_1(l),\theta_2(l')})_{l,l'}\overset d=(s_{l,l'})_{l,l'}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:subseq}
As in Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subarray}: compare finite-dimensional marginals and extend the restrictions of $\theta_1,\theta_2$ to the finitely many rows and columns involved to bijections $e_1,e_2$ of $\mathbb N$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The analogue of Lemma~1.6 for rectangles of cells]\label{lem:sg:lemma16-sep}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_enum_of_infinite_first,
        SpinGlasses.condExp_comp_ae_eq_condExp_compl_of_separately_exchangeable}
  \leanok
Let $(s_{l,l'})$ be separately exchangeable, $V_1,V_2\subseteq\mathbb N$ infinite, $j\in V_1$, $j'\in V_2$, $t=(j,j')$ and $f$ bounded measurable. Let $C$ be a set of cells with $t\notin C$ that contains every cell $c\ne t$ of $V_1\times V_2$. Then $\mathbb E[f(s_t)\mid\mathscr F_C]=\mathbb E[f(s_t)\mid\mathscr F_{\{c\ne t\}}]$ almost surely.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:exhaustion, lem:sg:l2proj}
Exactly as for Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma16}: enumerate $V_1,V_2$ and $\mathbb N$ injectively starting from $j$, $j'$, exhaust the cells of $V_1\times V_2$ other than $t$ by the cells in the first $n+1$ rows and columns, and use bijections $e_1,e_2$ of $\mathbb N$ matching the first $n+1$ enumerated rows, resp.\ columns (they fix $j$ and $j'$) to compare the joint laws with $s_t$; then Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:exhaustion} and the sandwich of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:l2proj}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The analogue of Eq.~(1.94) for separately exchangeable arrays]\label{thm:sg:cond-indep-array-sep}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.condExp_prod_comp_eq_prod_condExp_of_separately_exchangeable}
  \leanok
Let $K$ be a set of cells, $t$ a finite index set and $l:t\to(\mathbb N\times\mathbb N)\setminus K$ injective. Suppose every cell $l(i)=(j,j')$ has infinite $V_1\ni j$, $V_2\ni j'$ such that every cell $c\ne l(i)$ of $V_1\times V_2$ lies in $K$. Then for bounded measurable $f_i$, $\mathbb E[\prod_{i\in t}f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_K]=\prod_{i\in t}\mathbb E[f_i(s_{l(i)})\mid\mathscr F_K]$ almost surely.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:seq-decoupling, lem:sg:lemma16-sep}
The same argument as for Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:cond-indep-array}, with Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma16-sep} in place of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma16}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Aldous--Hoover representation of separately exchangeable arrays, Theorem~1.4 of the book, and its converse]\label{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-sep}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_injective_fix_even,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_vertexData_comp_inj,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_rowColPair,
        SpinGlasses.measurable_oddEntry,
        SpinGlasses.range_vertexRow,
        SpinGlasses.range_vertexCol,
        SpinGlasses.comap_rowData,
        SpinGlasses.comap_colData,
        SpinGlasses.comap_rowColPair,
        SpinGlasses.comap_rowColData,
        SpinGlasses.infinite_vertexSet_sep,
        SpinGlasses.cells_rowColData,
        SpinGlasses.condExp_rowColData_ae_eq_integral,
        SpinGlasses.map_rowColData_eq_compProd,
        SpinGlasses.map_rowColData_eq_coded,
        SpinGlasses.exists_aldousHoover_representation_separately,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_aldousHoover_separately_comp_equiv}
  \leanok
Let $(s_{l,l'})_{l,l'\in\mathbb N}$ be a separately exchangeable array with values in a nonempty standard Borel space $E$. Then there is a measurable $\sigma:[0,1]^4\to E$ with
\[(s_{l,l'})_{l,l'}\overset d=\big(\sigma(w,u_l,v_{l'},x_{l,l'})\big)_{l,l'},\]
where $w$, $(u_l)$, $(v_{l'})$ and $(x_{l,l'})_{(l,l')\in\mathbb N^2}$ are independent and uniform on $[0,1]$. Conversely, for every measurable $\sigma$ the array on the right is separately exchangeable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:de-finetti, thm:sg:cond-indep-array-sep, lem:sg:lemma16-sep, lem:sg:subarray-sep, lem:sg:iid-split, lem:sg:cond-iid, lem:sg:coding, lem:sg:mixture}
For the odd row $2a+1$ let $S_a$ be the entries of this row against the even columns together with the block of even rows and even columns (reindexed by $\mathbb N^2$), and for the odd column $2b+1$ let $T_b$ be the entries of this column against the even rows together with the same block; let $Q_{a,b}=s_{2a+1,2b+1}$. Injections of the odd rows and of the odd columns extend to injections of $\mathbb N$ fixing the even numbers, so Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subarray-sep} shows that $\big((S_a)_a,(T_b)_b,(Q_{a,b})_{a,b}\big)$ keeps its law when $S$ is composed with an injection $\theta_1$, $T$ with an injection $\theta_2$ and $Q$ with $\theta_1\times\theta_2$.
(1) \emph{Common noise.} The sequence of pairs $(S_k,T_k)_k$ is exchangeable with values in a standard Borel space, so by de Finetti's theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:de-finetti}) $(S_k,T_k)_k\overset d=(G(w,u_k))_k$, $G=(X,Y)$. Taking the subsequences $k=2a$ for $S$ and $k=2b+1$ for $T$ (which does not change the law, by the invariance just noted) and applying Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:iid-split} gives $\big((S_a)_a,(T_b)_b\big)\overset d=\big((X(w,u_a))_a,(Y(w,v_b))_b\big)$ with $w,(u_a),(v_b)$ independent: this is where the independence of the row and the column noise comes from. (In the book this step is carried out in the course of the proof of Theorem~1.4.)
(2) \emph{Conditional independence.} Let $\mathscr F=\sigma(S_a,T_b:a,b)$; it is generated by the cells with an even row or an even column. For $a,b$ the cells of $\{2a+1,\text{even}\}\times\{2b+1,\text{even}\}$ other than $(2a+1,2b+1)$ are generated by $S_a$ and $T_b$ together and lie in the cells generating $\mathscr F$, which do not contain $(2a+1,2b+1)$. Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma16-sep} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:cond-indep-array-sep} show that the $Q_{a,b}$ are conditionally independent given $\mathscr F$ and $\mathbb E[h(Q_{a,b})\mid\mathscr F]=\int h\,d\nu(S_a,T_b)$ with a kernel $\nu$ which is the same for all $(a,b)$ because $(S_a,T_b,Q_{a,b})\overset d=(S_0,T_0,Q_{0,0})$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-iid}(a) the law of $\big((S,T),Q\big)$ is $\mathrm{law}(S,T)\otimes K$, $K(x,y)=\bigotimes_{(a,b)}\nu(x_a,y_b)$.
(3) \emph{Coding and assembly.} Code $\nu$ by a uniform variable, $\nu(p,\cdot)=\mathrm{Leb}\circ H(p,\cdot)^{-1}$, and put $\sigma(w,u,v,x)=H\big((X(w,u),Y(w,v)),x\big)$. By (1) and (2) and the coded-cells lemma behind Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover} (no symmetry of $\nu$ is needed here, unlike for weakly exchangeable arrays) the pair of vertex data and the array $\sigma(w,u_a,v_b,x_{a,b})$ has the law $\mathrm{law}(S,T)\otimes K$, so $Q\overset d=\big(\sigma(w,u_a,v_b,x_{a,b})\big)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subarray-sep} with $\theta_1(l)=\theta_2(l)=2l+1$ the array $Q$ has the law of the whole array.
The converse: permuting rows and columns by $e_1,e_2$ permutes $(u_l)$, $(v_{l'})$ and $(x_{l,l'})$ (by $e_1$, $e_2$ and $e_1\times e_2$), which does not change their law (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:mixture}).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Deviations from the plan and from Panchenko's text, and what is left]\label{rem:sg:aldous-hoover-deviation}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Scope.} The three general tools, the statements about exchangeable \emph{sequences} (Eq.~(1.84), Lemma~1.5, Eq.~(1.88), Theorem~1.6 and its converse) and about weakly exchangeable \emph{arrays} (Eq.~(1.89), Lemma~1.6, Eq.~(1.94), Theorem~1.5 and its converse) are formalized. The Aldous--Hoover representation of separately exchangeable arrays (Theorem~1.4) and its converse, and the transfer of Theorem~1.5 to curried and Gram-type arrays (Theorems~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-curried} and~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-gram}), are formalized as well. Eqs.~(1.78)--(1.79) and~(1.95)--(1.96) as separate statements are not, and no Lean declaration asserts them. The fact that positive semidefiniteness removes the cell noise, in the form of the Dovbysh--Sudakov theorem, is the subject of Section~\ref{sec:sg:dovbysh-sudakov}.
\item \emph{Norm equality as an integral.} The equality case of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:l2proj} is stated with $\int(\mathbb E[f\mid\mathscr M_i])^2$ rather than with the $L^2$ norms in the book, because this is the form in which the exchangeability arguments produce it.
\item \emph{Hypothesis only for the functions used.} The book assumes the conditioning identity for all bounded continuous $f$; Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:seq-decoupling} needs it only for the functions that occur in the product, and the filtration $\mathscr G_k$ is arbitrary.
It is the forward half of Mathlib's characterization of conditional independence (\texttt{iCondIndepFun\_iff}), which requires a standard Borel ambient space that is not needed here.
\item \emph{Exchangeability under all bijections.} Panchenko's Eq.~(1.81) uses finite permutations; we assume invariance of the law of the whole sequence under every bijection of $\mathbb N$ (see the paragraph on exchangeable sequences). The two are equivalent but the equivalence is not formalized.
\item \emph{Bounded measurable test functions and a standard Borel state space.} Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma15} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:cond-indep} are stated for bounded \emph{measurable} $f$ (the book takes bounded continuous $f$; nothing in the proof needs continuity, and this avoids any approximation step), and the sequence takes values in an arbitrary nonempty standard Borel space instead of $\mathbb R$; the latter is what the array case needs, where the de Finetti theorem is applied to a sequence of arrays.
\item \emph{Eq.~(1.88) for arbitrary infinite $K$.} The book states it for a set $I$ with infinite complement and indices in $I$; Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:cond-indep} takes any infinite $K$ and distinct indices outside $K$. Lemma~1.5 is proved through the equality of $L^2$ norms (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma15}(a)) and Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:l2proj}, as in the book.
\item \emph{The mixture is identified directly.} The book concludes de Finetti's theorem by representing the conditional law; here the identification of the law of $(Y_i)$ with the mixture $\int\kappa^{\otimes\mathbb N}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-iid}) is done on measurable boxes. For this the kernel form of the coding lemma from Mathlib (\texttt{Kernel.exists\_measurable\_map\_eq\_unitInterval}) is used directly; the transport statement \texttt{identDistrib\_coding} of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:coding} is not needed for sequences (it is meant for the array case, where the pair of an array entry and the vertex noise must be coded).
\item \emph{The coding lemma is not proved from scratch.} The book builds the quantile function by hand; here the existence of the kernel and of its uniform representation are Mathlib's \texttt{condDistrib} and \texttt{Kernel.exists\_measurable\_map\_eq\_unitInterval}, and the new content is the assembly into an identity of laws.
\item \emph{Arrays are indexed by unordered pairs.} The book writes a symmetric array as $(s_{l,l'})_{l,l'}$ with $s_{l,l'}=s_{l',l}$ and the noise $x_{l,l'}=x_{l',l}$; we index both by $\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)$, so that no symmetry hypothesis is needed. Weak exchangeability is invariance under every bijection of the vertices acting on pairs (compare Remark item on exchangeability under bijections). The Lean statement of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover} compares the arrays as functions on ordered pairs $(l,l')$, the nesting of the noise is $((w,(u_l)),(x_c))$, and the values are in an arbitrary nonempty standard Borel space (the book has real values).
The pair-indexed form $\mathrm{IdentDistrib}(\omega\mapsto(p\mapsto s_{[(e p_1,e p_2)]}(\omega)),\,\omega\mapsto(p\mapsto s_{[p]}(\omega)))$ suggested for the overlap array is the same statement composed with $p\mapsto[p]$. The curried form $R:\Omega\to\mathbb N\to\mathbb N\to E$ used for the overlap array ($\Lambda\circ(A\mapsto(A_{e(l),e(l')}))^{-1}=\Lambda$) is bridged to the $\operatorname{Sym}^2$-indexed form by Theorems~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-curried} and~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-gram}: the almost sure symmetry of $R$ is what allows $s$ to be defined on $\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)$. We index by unordered pairs because the weak-exchangeability hypothesis is naturally an invariance under bijections of the vertices acting on pairs, and a symmetric array indexed by ordered pairs would carry an extra symmetry constraint through every lemma.
\item \emph{Odd and even vertices; the set $I$.} The book fixes an arbitrary infinite set $I$ of vertices with infinite complement; we take $I$ to be the odd numbers. The vertex array of the odd vertex $2a+1$ is the sub-array on $\{2a+1\}\cup\{\text{even vertices}\}$, reindexed by $\kappa_a(0)=2a+1$, $\kappa_a(m+1)=2m$. The reindexing $\kappa$, the vertex arrays $S$ and the cell noise $Z$ are not Lean definitions: they are obtained inside the proof together with their defining equations, and every auxiliary lemma takes them as hypotheses with these equations, which keeps the lemmas reusable (for instance for separately exchangeable arrays). Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lemma16} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:cond-indep-array} are stated for arbitrary sets $C$, $K$ of pairs satisfying the sandwich condition, not only for the pairs inside $V$.
\item \emph{Symmetrization.} The book obtains a function $f$ symmetric in its two middle arguments by a symmetrization remark; here it is made explicit: the two vertex variables are ordered, $f(w,u,u',x)=H(X(w,u),X(w,u'),x)$ for $u\le u'$ and with the arguments swapped otherwise. This is legitimate because the conditional law $\nu$ of an entry given the two adjacent vertex arrays is symmetric almost everywhere (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-swap}); no explicit averaging of $f$ is needed.
\item \emph{Shared lemmas.} Lemmas~1.5 and~1.6 are derived from one exhaustion principle (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:exhaustion}) and Eqs.~(1.88) and~(1.94) from one decoupling statement for cells (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:seq-decoupling}); the conditional independence in the array case is proved through the general mixture statement of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cond-iid}(a), in which the coordinate laws $\kappa_i$ differ from coordinate to coordinate, the sequence case (b) being its special case.
\item \emph{The coding lemma, again.} For arrays the statement \texttt{identDistrib\_coding} of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:coding} is still not used: after fixing the kernel $\nu$ of an entry given the two adjacent vertex arrays, Mathlib's \texttt{Kernel.exists\_measurable\_map\_eq\_unitInterval} codes it directly.
\item \emph{Suggestions of the formalization review.} (i) Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover} indeed applies the de Finetti theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:de-finetti}) verbatim to the sequence of vertex arrays, with state space $E'=E^{\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)}$, which is standard Borel because $\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb N)$ is countable. (ii) The converse is a reduction to the invariance of an infinite product measure under a bijection of the index set, used for the vertex noise and for the cell noise; it is about 50 lines rather than the expected 20, mostly measurability of the maps and the pushforward of the nested product. (iii) The array-update lemma shared with the sequential-learning files was not needed, since no coordinate-update argument occurs. (iv) The lemma rewriting a conditional expectation through \texttt{HasCondDistrib} was not moved to a shared file: Mathlib's \texttt{condExp\_ae\_eq\_integral\_condDistrib} is already the one-line rewrite used here, none of the new statements mentions \texttt{HasCondDistrib}, and moving it would only touch the sequential-learning files.
\item \emph{Eq.~(1.96) is inlined.} The statement for unordered pairs, that the entry at $\{l,l'\}$ is a function of the two vertex arrays and the cell noise, is not a separate declaration; it is part of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover}. Also not formalized: the equivalence of finite-permutation and bijection weak exchangeability, and the unused transport lemma \texttt{identDistrib\_coding}.
\item \emph{Separately exchangeable arrays: the route and the hypotheses.} The hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-sep} is invariance under all pairs of bijections of $\mathbb N$ (not finitely supported ones), and the cell noise is indexed by $\mathbb N\times\mathbb N$ (no symmetry is needed). The noise is nested as $\big((w,((u_l),(v_{l'}))),(x_c)\big)$. The independence of the row noise $(u_l)$ and the column noise $(v_{l'})$ is obtained by applying de Finetti's theorem once to the sequence of pairs $(S_k,T_k)$ of a row and a column vertex array and then reading $S$ off the even and $T$ off the odd i.i.d.\ variables (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:iid-split}); I did not follow the book's order of arguments for this step and the book's text for Theorem~1.4 was not re-checked against it.
\item \emph{Curried and Gram-type arrays.} The transfer to curried arrays defines the cell array through $\min$ and $\max$ of the two indices, so almost sure symmetry is the only property used. Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-gram} uses only symmetry and the unit diagonal of the law; positive semidefiniteness and the bound $|A_{l,l'}|\le1$ are not used, and the function $f$ is not shown to be bounded by $1$ or to be independent of the cell noise.
\item \emph{Shared code.} The cell coding step is the generic Lemma \texttt{map\_codedCells\_eq\_compProd} (kernel of the cells given any vertex data, for any index set of cells), used for weakly and for separately exchangeable arrays; the injection-invariance of the vertex data is obtained from the sub-array lemma for $s$ itself (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:subarray-sep}), by extending an injection of the odd numbers to an injection of $\mathbb N$ fixing the even numbers.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{The Dovbysh--Sudakov representation}\label{sec:sg:dovbysh-sudakov}

Section~1.5 of the book proves that an exchangeable random array $R=(R_{l,l'})$ which is almost surely positive semidefinite is, in law, the Gram array of an i.i.d.\ sample from a random measure on a Hilbert space, plus a nonnegative diagonal (Theorem~1.7).
This is the structure theorem for the overlap array of Section~1.3: it says that the asymptotic overlap law is the law of the overlaps of replicas drawn from a (random) asymptotic Gibbs measure.
The proof has one idea: positive semidefiniteness lets one attach to $R$ a \emph{Gaussian array} $g_{l,i}$ whose columns are conditionally i.i.d.\ Gaussian sequences with covariance $R$; this array is separately exchangeable, so the Aldous--Hoover representation of Theorem~1.4 applies to it; and the strong law of large numbers recovers $R$ from $g$ as the limit of the empirical covariances $n^{-1}\sum_{i\le n}g_{l,i}g_{l',i}$, which on the Aldous--Hoover side are computed by integrating out the cell noise.
The files are \texttt{SpinGlasses/DovbyshSudakov.lean} (the theorems), \texttt{DovbyshSudakovGaussian.lean} (the Gaussian array) and \texttt{DovbyshSudakovLimit.lean} (the Aldous--Hoover side), with general-purpose inputs in \texttt{ForMathlib}.
Deviations are listed in Remark~\ref{rem:sg:dovbysh-sudakov-deviation}.

\begin{lemma}[Cholesky factorization of an infinite positive semidefinite array]\label{lem:sg:cholesky}
  \lean{Matrix.cholInf,
        Matrix.measurable_cholInf,
        Matrix.sum_cholInf_mul_eq,
        Matrix.sum_cholInf_mul_eq_dotProduct,
        Matrix.inner_cholInf_realization,
        Matrix.posSemidef_symm,
        Matrix.posSemidef_comp}
  \leanok
Let $A:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N\to\mathbb R$ be such that every finite principal submatrix is positive semidefinite. The lower triangular array $L=\operatorname{cholInf}(A)$, defined row by row, satisfies $\sum_{j<K}L_{l,j}L_{l',j}=A_{l,l'}$ for $K>\max(l,l')$, and every entry $L_{l,k}$ is a measurable function of $A$. If $f:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$ is any map, the array $(A_{f(l),f(l')})$ again has positive semidefinite finite principal submatrices.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
For $k<l$ put $L_{l,k}=(A_{l,k}-\sum_{j<k}L_{l,j}L_{k,j})/L_{k,k}$ (and $0$ if the pivot $L_{k,k}$ vanishes), $L_{l,l}=\sqrt{A_{l,l}-\sum_{j<l}L_{l,j}^2}$ and $L_{l,k}=0$ for $k>l$. Measurability follows by induction along the recursion. For the factorization one shows by induction on $m$ that the residual $A_{l,l'}-\sum_{j<m}L_{l,j}L_{l',j}$ is a positive semidefinite form on the indices $\ge m$: removing a positive pivot is a rank one downdate (completing the square in the $m$-th variable), and below a zero pivot the whole column of the residual vanishes, by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality for the positive semidefinite form.
Hence the residual at the diagonal is a square and the one below a zero pivot vanishes, which is the identity. The last statement is the fact that a principal submatrix along any map of indices is a submatrix of a finite principal submatrix.
\end{proof}

The Cholesky factor is not in Mathlib (its \texttt{LDL} decomposition needs positive definite matrices) and a zero pivot cannot be excluded for a random array. The point of an \emph{explicit, measurable} factor is that the Gaussian array can be written as a finite sum of i.i.d.\ Gaussians, with the covariance as a measurable parameter: no Kolmogorov extension theorem and no measurable selection are needed.

\begin{lemma}[The Gaussian sequence with a prescribed covariance]\label{lem:sg:gaussian-gram}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.map_dotProduct_eq_of_gram_eq,
        ProbabilityTheory.map_cholInf_comp_equiv,
        ProbabilityTheory.integrable_integral_cholInf_mul}
  \leanok
Let $(c_k)_k$ be i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian and $A$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cholesky}. The sequence $Y_l=\sum_{k\le l}L_{l,k}c_k$ is centered with $\mathbb E[Y_lY_{l'}]=A_{l,l'}$, and $Y_lY_{l'}$ is integrable. For a permutation $e$ of $\mathbb N$ the sequence $(Y_{e(l)})_l$ has the same law as the Gaussian sequence built from the array $(A_{e(l),e(l')})$, although the two Cholesky factors differ.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:cholesky}
Linear forms of i.i.d.\ Gaussians with the same Gram matrix have the same law (a centered Gaussian vector is determined by its covariance; this is in the repository). The two laws on $\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}$ are compared on finite sets of coordinates, which determine a measure on $\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}$; on a finite set of coordinates both are linear forms of the first $N$ Gaussians whose Gram matrix is $(A_{e(l),e(l')})$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cholesky}, applied to $A$ and to the permuted array.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The Gaussian array of an exchangeable positive semidefinite array]\label{thm:sg:gaussian-array}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.identDistrib_prod_comp_of_ae_map_eq,
        SpinGlasses.identDistrib_gaussianArray_separately,
        SpinGlasses.ae_tendsto_gaussianArray_mean}
  \leanok
Let $R$ be a random array, almost surely with positive semidefinite finite principal submatrices, whose law is invariant under $R\mapsto(R_{e(l),e(l')})$ for every bijection $e$. Let $\xi=(\xi_{i,k})$ be i.i.d.\ standard Gaussians independent of $R$ and $g_{l,i}=\sum_{k\le l}\operatorname{cholInf}(R)_{l,k}\xi_{i,k}$. Then $(g_{l,i})_{l,i}$ is separately exchangeable, and almost surely $n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}g_{l,i}g_{l',i}\to R_{l,l'}$ for all $l,l'$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:gaussian-gram}
Permuting the columns permutes the i.i.d.\ columns of $\xi$, whose law is invariant. Permuting the rows by $e$: given $R$, the law of the permuted array is the law of the array built from $(R_{e(l),e(l')})$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:gaussian-gram}, columns being independent), and $(R_{e(l),e(l')})\overset d=R$ by hypothesis; the general statement that an equivariant function of a parameter and an independent noise keeps its law when the parameter's law is invariant is Lemma \texttt{identDistrib\_prod\_comp\_of\_ae\_map\_eq}. For the limit, given $R$ the columns are i.i.d.\ with $\mathbb E[g_{l,i}g_{l',i}\mid R]=R_{l,l'}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:gaussian-gram}), and Etemadi's strong law (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:slln}) applies conditionally.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Strong laws for i.i.d.\ sequences, conditionally i.i.d.\ sequences and blocks of an i.i.d.\ family]\label{lem:sg:slln}
  \lean{ProbabilityTheory.ae_tendsto_mean_infinitePi,
        ProbabilityTheory.ae_tendsto_mean_prod_infinitePi,
        ProbabilityTheory.ae_tendsto_mean_infinitePi_blocks,
        ProbabilityTheory.ae_tendsto_atTop_mean_infinitePi_blocks}
  \leanok
(a) If $(c_i)$ are i.i.d.\ with law $\nu$ and $g$ is $\nu$-integrable, then $n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}g(c_i)\to\int g\,d\nu$ almost surely. More generally, if $a\sim\mu$ and $(c_i)$ are i.i.d.\ with law $\nu$, independent of $a$, and $\Psi(a,\cdot)$ is $\nu$-integrable for almost every $a$, then $n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}\Psi(a,c_i)\to\int\Psi(a,c)\,d\nu(c)$ almost surely.
(b) Let $\sigma=(\sigma_j)_{j\in J}$ be i.i.d.\ with law $\nu$ and $f_i:F\to J$ injective maps ($F$ finite) with pairwise disjoint ranges. If $\Phi$ is integrable for $\nu^{\otimes F}$ then $n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}\Phi(\sigma\circ f_i)\to\int\Phi\,d\nu^{\otimes F}$ almost surely.
(c) If $\Phi\ge0$ is \emph{not} integrable, then $n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}\Phi(\sigma\circ f_i)\to\infty$ almost surely.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The first statement of (a) is Etemadi's strong law (Mathlib's \texttt{strong\_law\_ae}) for the pairwise independent, identically distributed variables $g(c_i)$; the second is Fubini and the first for each fixed $a$. (b) is the same law applied to the variables $\Phi(\sigma\circ f_i)$, which are identically distributed (the law of the restriction of an i.i.d.\ family to an injective block) and pairwise independent (restrictions to disjoint blocks, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:iid-split}). (c): apply (b) to the truncations $\min(\Phi,M)$, which are bounded, and use that $\int\min(\Phi,M)\to\infty$ by monotone convergence.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Partial integrals of square-integrable functions]\label{lem:sg:jensen-partial}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.sq_integral_le_integral_sq,
        MeasureTheory.integrable_sq_integral_prod,
        MeasureTheory.integral_prod_mul_prod,
        MeasureTheory.ae_integrable_section_of_integrable_sq,
        MeasureTheory.memLp_integral_abs_of_integrable_sq,
        Measurable.integral_prod_right'}
  \leanok
Let $f,f'$ be measurable functions of $(v,x)$ that are square-integrable for $\mu_V\otimes\mu_X$ (probability measures), and $m(v)=\int f(v,x)\,d\mu_X(x)$, $m'$ likewise. Then $m^2$ is integrable and $\int m^2\le\iint f^2$; and, for two independent copies $x,x'$ of the cell variable, $\iiint f(v,x)f'(v,x')=\int m\,m'$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
Jensen's inequality $(\int f)^2\le\int f^2$ on each section ($\operatorname{Var}\ge0$), integrated in $v$. For the second identity, the integrand is integrable because $\int|f(v,\cdot)|$ and $\int|f'(v,\cdot)|$ are square-integrable in $v$ and the product of two square-integrable functions is integrable; then Fubini and $\int\!\int f(v,x)f'(v,x')=(\int f)(\int f')$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Distance to a finite span as a rational infimum]\label{lem:sg:proj-distance}
  \lean{infDist_span_range_sq_eq_iInf_rat,
        measurable_infDist_span_range_sq,
        ciInf_range_subtype}
  \leanok
Let $v,w_1,\dots,w_n$ be vectors of a real inner product space. Then
\[
\operatorname{dist}\bigl(v,\operatorname{span}(w_1,\dots,w_n)\bigr)^2=\inf_{c\in\mathbb Q^n}\Bigl(\langle v,v\rangle-2\sum_i c_i\langle v,w_i\rangle+\sum_{i,j}c_ic_j\langle w_i,w_j\rangle\Bigr),
\]
with no linear independence assumption on the $w_i$. Consequently, if $v,w_i$ depend measurably on a parameter in the sense that the inner products $\langle v,v\rangle,\langle v,w_i\rangle,\langle w_i,w_j\rangle$ are measurable functions of it, then the squared distance is measurable.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The squared distance is the infimum over real $c\in\mathbb R^n$ of the quadratic form $\|v-\sum_ic_iw_i\|^2$, which is continuous in $c$; the rational vectors are dense in $\mathbb R^n$, so the infimum over them is the same. Expanding the square gives the displayed form. A countable infimum of measurable functions is measurable. This lemma is not used in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov}. It was written for the measurable reconstruction of $\|h_l\|$ in Lemma~1.7, but the formal proof of Lemma~1.7 (Section~\ref{sec:sg:reconstruction}) does not need it: the quadratic form of the lemma is computed directly from the entries of the array $R$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:diag-residual}), so the jittered vectors are never constructed in an extended Hilbert space and no orthogonal projection has to be formed. It is kept as a stand-alone general fact.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Empirical covariances of an Aldous--Hoover array]\label{thm:sg:ah-empirical}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.measurePreserving_aldousHooverNoise,
        SpinGlasses.ae_tendsto_mean_aldousHoover_offDiag,
        SpinGlasses.ae_tendsto_mean_aldousHoover_diag,
        SpinGlasses.ae_integrable_section_of_ae_exists_tendsto,
        SpinGlasses.exists_ae_tendsto_mean_aldousHoover}
  \leanok
Let $\tau:[0,1]^4\to\mathbb R$ be measurable, $w$, $(u_l)$, $(v_i)$, $(x_{l,i})$ independent and uniform, and $s_{l,i}=\tau(w,u_l,v_i,x_{l,i})$.
(a) If $n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}s_{0,i}^2$ converges almost surely to a finite limit, then $\tau(w,a,\cdot,\cdot)$ is square-integrable for almost every $(w,a)$.
(b) In that case there are measurable $h:[0,1]^3\to\mathbb R$ with $h(w,a,\cdot)\in L^2(0,1)$ for every $(w,a)$ (the partial integral of $\tau$ over the last variable, set to $0$ on the null set where $\tau(w,a,\cdot,\cdot)$ is not square-integrable) and $a:[0,1]^2\to[0,\infty)$ such that, almost surely and for all $l,l'$,
\[\frac1n\sum_{i<n}s_{l,i}s_{l',i}\longrightarrow\int_0^1h(w,u_l,v)\,h(w,u_{l'},v)\,dv+\mathbf 1_{l=l'}\,a(w,u_l).\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:slln, lem:sg:jensen-partial, lem:sg:iid-split}
All the randomness except $(w,(u_l))$ is realized as one i.i.d.\ family $\sigma$ indexed by $\mathbb N\oplus\mathbb N\times\mathbb N$, with $v_i=\sigma_{\mathrm{inl}\,i}$ and $x_{l,i}=\sigma_{\mathrm{inr}(l,i)}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:iid-split}). Given $(w,u)$, the product $s_{l,i}s_{l',i}$ is a fixed function of the block $(\sigma_{\mathrm{inl}\,i},\sigma_{\mathrm{inr}(l,i)},\sigma_{\mathrm{inr}(l',i)})$ and these blocks are pairwise disjoint in $i$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:slln}(b) gives the limit as the mean of the block. For $l\ne l'$ the cell variables $x_{l,i},x_{l',i}$ are independent copies, and the mean is $\int m\,m'$ with $m(v)=\int\tau(w,u_l,v,x)\,dx$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:jensen-partial}); for $l=l'$ it is $\iint\tau^2$, and $a=\iint\tau^2-\int m^2\ge0$ by Jensen. (a) is Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:slln}(c) applied to $\Phi=\tau(w,u_0,\cdot,\cdot)^2$: if the section were not integrable for a set of $(w,u_0)$ of positive measure, the averages would tend to $\infty$ with positive probability.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Dovbysh--Sudakov representation, Theorem~1.7 of the book]\label{thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_dovbysh_sudakov_representation}
  \leanok
Let $R=(R_{l,l'})$ be a random array whose finite principal submatrices are almost surely positive semidefinite and whose law is invariant under $R\mapsto(R_{e(l),e(l')})$ for every bijection $e$ of $\mathbb N$. Then there are measurable $h:[0,1]^3\to\mathbb R$ with $h(w,u,\cdot)\in L^2(0,1)$ for all $(w,u)$ and $a:[0,1]^2\to[0,\infty)$ such that, as arrays indexed by $\mathbb N\times\mathbb N$,
\[R_{l,l'}\overset d=\int_0^1h(w,u_l,v)\,h(w,u_{l'},v)\,dv+\mathbf 1_{l=l'}\,a(w,u_l),\]
with $w$ and $(u_l)$ independent and uniform on $[0,1]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:gaussian-array, thm:sg:aldous-hoover-sep, thm:sg:ah-empirical}
Take the Gaussian array $g$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:gaussian-array}; it is separately exchangeable, so $g\overset d=(\tau(w,u_l,v_i,x_{l,i}))$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:aldous-hoover-sep}. The map sending an array $y$ to the matrix of limits $\limsup_n n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}y_{l,i}y_{l',i}$ is measurable. Applied to $g$ it returns $R$ almost surely (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:gaussian-array}); applied to the Aldous--Hoover array it returns, almost surely, the matrix of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:ah-empirical}, whose hypothesis (a) holds because the limit exists for $g$, hence (equality in law, applied to the measurable event ``the averages $n^{-1}\sum_{i<n}y_{0,i}^2$ converge'') for the Aldous--Hoover array. Equality in law of $g$ and the Aldous--Hoover array gives equality in law of the two matrices of limits.
\end{proof}

The variable $w$ is the randomness of the random measure: conditionally on $w$, the pairs $(h(w,u_l,\cdot),a(w,u_l))$ are i.i.d.\ with a law $\eta(w)$ on $L^2(0,1)\times[0,\infty)$, so $R$ is, in law, the Gram array of an i.i.d.\ sample from a random measure plus an independent diagonal.

\begin{theorem}[Dovbysh--Sudakov representation of a Gram-type law]\label{thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov-gram}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_dovbysh_sudakov_representation_of_gram,
        SpinGlasses.exists_dovbysh_sudakov_representation_of_gram_set,
        SpinGlasses.map_map_curry_array}
  \leanok
Let $\Lambda$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb R^{\mathbb N\times\mathbb N}$ concentrated on arrays with unit diagonal and positive semidefinite finite principal submatrices, and invariant under $A\mapsto(A_{e(l),e(l')})$. Then there is a measurable $h:[0,1]^3\to\mathbb R$ with $\int_0^1h(w,u,v)^2\,dv\le1$ for every $(w,u)$ such that $\Lambda$ is the law of
\[\Big(\mathbf 1_{l=l'}+\mathbf 1_{l\ne l'}\int_0^1h(w,u_l,v)\,h(w,u_{l'},v)\,dv\Big)_{l,l'},\]
with $w$ and $(u_l)$ i.i.d.\ uniform. The same holds if $\Lambda$ is only assumed to give measure $1$ to the (closed) set of Gram-type arrays of Section~\ref{sec:sg:asymptotic-gibbs}; in particular it applies to every limit produced by Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:asymptotic-overlap}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov} to the identity array on $(\mathbb R^{\mathbb N\times\mathbb N},\Lambda)$. The diagonal of the representing array equals $1$ almost surely, so $\int h^2+a=1$ with $a\ge0$, which gives $\int h^2\le1$ for almost every $(w,u_l)$; setting $h=0$ where this fails changes the array only on a null set. For the Gram set, closedness gives measurability, so measure $1$ is the almost sure statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Deviations from the book and what is left]\label{rem:sg:dovbysh-sudakov-deviation}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The Hilbert space.} The book represents the array through a random measure $\eta$ on $L^2(0,1)\times[0,\infty)$. The Lean statements of this section give the Gram array through the functions $h(w,u,\cdot)\in L^2(0,1)$ and the integral $\int h\,h'$. That $(w,u)\mapsto h(w,u,\cdot)$ is a measurable map into the Hilbert space $L^2(0,1)$, and hence that the pushforward measures form a random measure, is proved in Section~\ref{sec:sg:gibbs-measure} (for the marginal $G(w)$ on $L^2(0,1)$; the pairs with the diagonal part $a$ are not treated there). It does \emph{not} need Lemma~1.7: for a jointly measurable $h$ the map $q\mapsto[h(q,\cdot)]\in L^2(0,1)$ is measurable because its distance to every point of $L^2(0,1)$ is a measurable function of $q$ (Fubini) and $L^2(0,1)$ is separable (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lp-section}). We state the representation through $h$ rather than through $\eta(w)$ because the book's own argument produces $h$ first (as a partial integral of the Aldous--Hoover function), and because a statement about $\eta(w)$ needs a $\sigma$-algebra on $L^2$ that Mathlib lacks.
\item \emph{Lemma~1.7} (that $\eta$ is a measurable function of $R$ up to an isometry) is formalized in Section~\ref{sec:sg:reconstruction}, for a fixed law $\eta$ on a separable Hilbert space. Its combination with the representation above, i.e.\ the asymptotic Gibbs measure on the unit ball of $L^2(0,1)$, is Section~\ref{sec:sg:gibbs-measure}; the measure there is built directly from $h$, so Lemma~1.7 is not used.
\item \emph{No integrability assumption.} The book does not assume $\mathbb E[R_{0,0}]<\infty$ and neither do the Lean statements. The strong law for the Aldous--Hoover array needs square-integrable sections; this is not assumed but derived (Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:ah-empirical}(a)) from the nonnegative strong law, Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:slln}(c), which the book does not discuss.
\item \emph{Another route to the Gaussian array.} The book generates, conditionally on $R$, a Gaussian vector with covariance $R$ and takes independent copies. Here it is $g_{l,i}=\sum_{k\le l}L_{l,k}\xi_{i,k}$ with the explicit measurable Cholesky factor $L$ of $R$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cholesky}); the only place where the exchangeability of $R$ enters is that the law of $g$ given $R$ depends on $R$ only through its entries, even though the Cholesky factors of $R$ and of a permuted $R$ are different matrices.
\item \emph{Order of limits.} The map ``limit of the empirical covariances'' is a $\limsup$ and is only meaningful on the almost sure set where the limits exist; this is why the comparison of the two arrays goes through a measurable map and an almost sure equality on both sides, rather than through conditional expectations as in the book's informal statement.
\item \emph{Reuse.} The same exchangeability transfer for an equivariant function of a parameter and a noise (Lemma \texttt{identDistrib\_prod\_comp\_of\_ae\_map\_eq}) and the product regroupings are general-purpose lemmas; the strong laws of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:slln} and the partial-integral lemmas of Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:jensen-partial} do not mention spin glasses.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Measurable reconstruction of the random measure (Lemma 1.7)}\label{sec:sg:reconstruction}

Lemma~1.7 of the book says that the random measure $\eta$ of the Dovbysh--Sudakov representation is not an artifact of the proof: if $(h_l,a_l)_{l\ge0}$ are i.i.d.\ from $\eta$ and $R_{l,l'}=\langle h_l,h_{l'}\rangle+\mathbf 1_{l=l'}a_l$, then $\eta$ is, up to an isometry of the Hilbert space, a measurable function of the array $R$ alone.
We formalize it for a fixed law $\eta$ on $H\times\mathbb R$, $H$ a separable Hilbert space, which is the situation of the lemma conditionally on the mixing variable $w$.
The idea of the proof is that the diagonal residual $a_0$ is the squared distance from $g_0=(h_0,\sqrt{a_0}\,e_0)$ to the span of the other $g_l=(h_l,\sqrt{a_l}\,e_l)$, with $e_l$ orthonormal, because $h_0$ is a cluster point of the sample and averaging kills the orthonormal jitter; that distance is a countable infimum of explicit functions of the entries of $R$.
Then $\|h_l\|^2$ and all inner products $\langle h_l,h_{l'}\rangle$ are measurable functions of $R$, the vectors $h_l$ are re-created from their Gram matrix by a Cholesky factorization, and $\eta$ is the weak limit of the empirical measures of the re-created points.
The Lean files are \texttt{SpinGlasses/DovbyshSudakovReconstruction.lean}, with the general inputs \texttt{ForMathlib/Probability/EmpiricalConvergence.lean}, \texttt{ForMathlib/MeasureTheory/Measure/ProbabilityMeasureMeasurable.lean} and \texttt{ForMathlib/Analysis/InnerProductSpace/GramIsometry.lean}.

\begin{lemma}[Empirical measures of an i.i.d.\ sample (Varadarajan); the sample is dense in the support]\label{lem:sg:empirical}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.map_empiricalMeasure,
        ProbabilityTheory.ae_tendsto_empiricalMeasure,
        MeasureTheory.mapClusterPt_of_tendsto_empiricalMeasure,
        ProbabilityTheory.ae_forall_mapClusterPt_of_mem_support,
        MeasureTheory.empiricalMeasure_apply,
        MeasureTheory.measure_isOpen_eq_iSup_finset_biUnion}
  \leanok
Let $X$ be a second countable metrizable space with its Borel $\sigma$-algebra and $x_0,x_1,\dots$ i.i.d.\ with law $\nu$. Then, almost surely, the empirical measures $(n+1)^{-1}\sum_{i\le n}\delta_{x_i}$ converge weakly to $\nu$. If the empirical measures of a deterministic sequence $(x_i)$ converge weakly to $\nu$, every point of the support of $\nu$ is a cluster point of $(x_i)$. Consequently, almost surely, every point of the support of $\nu$ is the limit of a subsequence of the sample.
Pushing an empirical measure forward by a measurable map is the empirical measure of the image points.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:slln}
Fix a countable base of the topology and let $\mathcal U$ be the countable family of finite unions of base sets. By the strong law (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:slln}(a)) applied to the indicators, almost surely $\mu_n(U)\to\nu(U)$ for all $U\in\mathcal U$ simultaneously, where $\mu_n$ is the empirical measure. An open set $G$ is the directed union of the members of $\mathcal U$ it contains, so $\nu(G)=\sup\{\nu(U):U\in\mathcal U,U\subseteq G\}\le\liminf_n\mu_n(G)$; the portmanteau theorem gives weak convergence. For the second statement let $V$ be an open neighbourhood of a point of the support, so $\nu(V)>0$. If $x_i\notin V$ for all large $i$, then $\mu_n(V)\le N/(n+1)\to0$ for a fixed $N$, contradicting $\nu(V)\le\liminf\mu_n(V)$ (portmanteau again).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A measurable limit of empirical measures]\label{lem:sg:measurable-limit}
  \lean{MeasureTheory.measurable_empiricalMeasure,
        MeasureTheory.continuous_toProbabilityMeasure_empiricalMeasure,
        MeasureTheory.ProbabilityMeasure.measurable_coe_borel,
        MeasureTheory.ProbabilityMeasure.measurable_of_borel,
        MeasureTheory.exists_measurable_tendsto_empiricalMeasure}
  \leanok
Let $X$ be a Polish space and $y_i:B\to X$ measurable random points on a measurable space $B$. The set of $b\in B$ for which the empirical measures $(n+1)^{-1}\sum_{i\le n}\delta_{y_i(b)}$ converge weakly is measurable, and there is a measurable map $g:B\to\mathcal P(X)$ (for the Giry $\sigma$-algebra) such that the empirical measures converge to $g(b)$ at every such $b$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
