If $\mathcal P(X)$ were known to be completely metrizable the first claim would follow from the measurability of the set where a sequence of measurable maps into a completely metrizable space converges; Mathlib does not have this for $\mathcal P(X)$, so we embed $X$ into the Hilbert cube $Q=[0,1]^{\mathbb N}$ (Mathlib) by $\iota$; $\iota$ is a measurable embedding because $X$ is Polish. The space $\mathcal P(Q)$ is compact and metrizable, hence completely metrizable, so by theorems of Mathlib the set of $b$ where the empirical measures of the points $\iota(y_i(b))$ converge in $\mathcal P(Q)$ is measurable and the limit $L(b)$ is a strongly measurable function of $b$ (the empirical measure of finitely many points is a continuous function of the points). The empirical measures of the $y_i(b)$ converge weakly in $\mathcal P(X)$ if and only if those of $\iota(y_i(b))$ converge to a limit $L(b)$ with $L(b)(\iota(X))=1$: one direction is continuity of $\iota_*$, the other is the portmanteau theorem, since every open subset of $X$ is $\iota^{-1}$ of an open subset of $Q$. The set of such $b$ is measurable ($\iota(X)$ is Borel), and the limit in $\mathcal P(X)$ is the pull-back $L(b)\circ\iota$ along the measurable embedding, which is measurable in $b$ because $b\mapsto L(b)(S)$ is Borel measurable for Borel $S$ (evaluations are Borel measurable for the weak topology: for closed $S$ they are limits of integrals of thickened indicators, and closed sets form a generating $\pi$-system).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Families with equal Gram matrices are related by an isometry]\label{lem:sg:gram-isometry}
  \lean{exists_linearIsometry_of_inner_eq, norm_sum_smul_sq}
  \leanok
Let $h_i\in H$ and $x_i\in H'$ be vectors of real Hilbert spaces with $\langle x_i,x_j\rangle=\langle h_i,h_j\rangle$ for all $i,j$. Then there is a linear isometry $U:H\to H\oplus H'$ (orthogonal direct sum) with $U(h_i)=(0,x_i)$ for all $i$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
The norm of a finite linear combination $\|\sum c_ix_i\|^2=\sum c_ic_j\langle x_i,x_j\rangle=\|\sum c_ih_i\|^2$ depends only on the Gram matrix, so $\sum c_ih_i\mapsto\sum c_ix_i$ is a well defined isometry from the span of the $h_i$ and extends to the closure $S$ of the span (Mathlib's \texttt{LinearMap.extendOfIsometry}). On $S^\perp$ take the identity into the first summand: $U(v)=(P_{S^\perp}v,\,V P_Sv)$. An isometry $H\to H'$ need not exist, because $S^\perp$ may be larger than the orthogonal complement of the span of the $x_i$; this is why $H$ is added to the target.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The residuals $a_l$ and the Gram matrix of the $h_l$ are measurable functions of $R$]\label{lem:sg:diag-residual}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_measurable_diag_residual,
        SpinGlasses.exists_measurable_gram_recovery}
  \leanok
Let $(h_l,a_l)_l$ be i.i.d.\ from a law $\eta$ on $H\times\mathbb R$ with $a\ge0$ $\eta$-almost surely, $H$ a separable Hilbert space, and $R_{l,l'}=\langle h_l,h_{l'}\rangle+\mathbf 1_{l=l'}a_l$. There are Borel functions $a'_l$ and $\Gamma_{l,l'}$ of the array such that, almost surely, $a'_l(R)=a_l$ and $\Gamma_{l,l'}(R)=\langle h_l,h_{l'}\rangle$ for all $l,l'$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:empirical}
For a finite set $s\subseteq\mathbb N$ and rational weights $c$ on $s$ put $Q_c(R)=R_{00}-2\sum_{j\in s}c_jR_{0,j+1}+\sum_{j,k\in s}c_jc_kR_{j+1,k+1}$, a measurable function of $R$ for each $c$. Since $0\ne j+1$ and the diagonal contributes only the terms $j=k$,
\[Q_c(R)=a_0+\Big\|h_0-\sum_{j}c_jh_{j+1}\Big\|^2+\sum_jc_j^2a_{j+1}\ \ge\ a_0.\]
(This is the squared distance of $g_0$ to $\sum c_jg_{j+1}$ for the jittered vectors $g_l$ described above, but they never have to be constructed.) Almost surely $(h_0,a_0)$ lies in the support of $\eta$ and hence, by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:empirical}, is a cluster point of the sample: for every $\delta>0$ and $m$ there are $m$ indices $j+1\ge1$ with $\|h_{j+1}-h_0\|<\delta$ and $a_{j+1}<a_0+\delta$. Taking $c_j=1/m$ on them gives $\|h_0-\sum c_jh_{j+1}\|\le\delta$ and $\sum c_j^2a_{j+1}\le(a_0+\delta)/m$, so $\inf_cQ_c(R)=a_0$. The infimum is over countably many $c$, so $A(R)=\inf_cQ_c(R)$ is Borel and $A(R)=a_0$ almost surely. Exchanging the indices $0$ and $l$ preserves the law of the sample, so $a'_l(R)=A(R_{\pi(\cdot),\pi(\cdot)})$ with $\pi=(0\ l)$ equals $a_l$ almost surely. Finally $\langle h_l,h_{l'}\rangle=R_{l,l'}$ for $l\ne l'$ and $\langle h_l,h_l\rangle=R_{l,l}-a'_l(R)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Measurable reconstruction of the random measure, Lemma~1.7 of the book]\label{thm:sg:reconstruction}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_measurable_dovbysh_reconstruction}
  \leanok
Let $H,H'$ be separable Hilbert spaces, $(\varepsilon_k)$ an orthonormal sequence in $H'$, $\eta$ a probability measure on $H\times\mathbb R$ with $a\ge0$ $\eta$-almost surely, $(h_l,a_l)_l$ i.i.d.\ from $\eta$ and $R_{l,l'}=\langle h_l,h_{l'}\rangle+\mathbf 1_{l=l'}a_l$. There is a measurable map $\eta'$ from arrays to probability measures on $(H\oplus H')\times\mathbb R$ such that, almost surely, there is a linear isometry $U:H\to H\oplus H'$ with
\[\eta'(R)=\text{the image of }\eta\text{ under }(h,a)\mapsto(Uh,a).\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:empirical, lem:sg:measurable-limit, lem:sg:gram-isometry, lem:sg:diag-residual, lem:sg:cholesky}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:diag-residual} the Gram matrix $\Gamma$ of the $h_l$ and the residuals $a_l$ are Borel functions of $R$ (almost surely). The matrix $\Gamma$ has positive semidefinite finite principal submatrices (it is a Gram matrix), so the rows $x_l=\sum_{k\le l}L_{l,k}\varepsilon_k$ of its Cholesky factor (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:cholesky}) satisfy $\langle x_l,x_{l'}\rangle=\Gamma_{l,l'}$ and are measurable functions of $R$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:gram-isometry} there is a linear isometry $U$ with $U(h_l)=(0,x_l)$. The empirical measures of $(h_l,a_l)$ converge weakly to $\eta$ almost surely (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:empirical}), so the empirical measures of the reconstructed points $((0,x_l),a_l)=(U h_l,a_l)$ converge weakly to the image of $\eta$ under $(h,a)\mapsto(Uh,a)$, by continuity of this map. The reconstructed points are measurable functions of $R$, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:measurable-limit} the weak limit is a measurable function $\eta'(R)$ at every $R$ where it exists; and limits are unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Differences from the book and what is left]\label{rem:sg:reconstruction-deviation}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Isometry into a larger space.} The book has an orthogonal transformation of $H$ onto itself. Our $U$ is a linear isometry of $H$ into $H\oplus H'$. A statement with an isometry $H\to H'$ (or a surjective one) can fail for the canonical choice of the re-created vectors, because the orthogonal complement of the span of the $h_l$ may have a different dimension from that of the span of the $x_l$; the extra summand makes the statement unconditional. For infinite-dimensional separable $H$ and $H'=H$ the space $H\oplus H'$ is isometric to $H$, but we do not use this. The law of the Gram array of an i.i.d.\ sample is the same for $\eta$ and for its image under $(h,a)\mapsto(Uh,a)$.
\item \emph{Proof route for the diagonal residual.} The idea (the residual $a_0$ is the squared distance from $g_0$ to the span of the other jittered vectors) is the one described at the beginning of the section. Instead of building the jittered vectors $g_l=(h_l,\sqrt{a_l}e_l)$ in the extended space $H\oplus\ell^2$ and projecting onto the closed span of infinitely many of them, we use the identity $Q_c(R)=a_0+\|h_0-\sum c_jh_{j+1}\|^2+\sum c_j^2a_{j+1}$ for the quadratic form of $R$ itself. Reason: the infimum over rational weights of an explicit function of the entries of $R$ is a countable infimum of measurable functions, so its measurability is immediate, while measurability of an infinite-dimensional orthogonal projection would have to be proved separately.
\item \emph{A fixed law.} The book uses Lemma~1.7 for the random measure $\eta(w)$; we prove it for a fixed $\eta$, conditionally on $w$. Reason: for fixed $\eta$ the proof is a statement about one i.i.d. sample, to which the strong law and Varadarajan's theorem apply directly; the random version needs these almost sure statements and the measurable choice of the limit uniformly in $w$, which we did not attempt because the asymptotic Gibbs measure of Section~\ref{sec:sg:gibbs-measure} does not need it. Combining it with the representation of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov} would require applying it to the random measure $\eta(w)$ on $L^2(0,1)\times[0,\infty)$ jointly in $w$; we have not done it, and Section~\ref{sec:sg:gibbs-measure} does not need it.
\item \emph{No moment assumption.} The reconstruction uses only that the support of $\eta$ is approached by the sample; no integrability of $\|h\|^2$ is assumed. Weak convergence refers to the norm topology of $H\oplus H'$.
\item \emph{Why a compactification.} The measurable choice of the weak limit (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:measurable-limit}) needs the set where the limit exists to be measurable. Mathlib does not know that the space of probability measures of a Polish space is completely metrizable, so we pass through the compact space $\mathcal P([0,1]^{\mathbb N})$. This is a stand-alone fact about empirical measures of random points in a Polish space.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{The asymptotic Gibbs measure on $L^2(0,1)$}\label{sec:sg:gibbs-measure}

This section completes the representation of Section~\ref{sec:sg:dovbysh-sudakov} for the SK model: the limit of the annealed overlap laws is the law of the overlap array of an i.i.d.\ sample from a random probability measure $G(w)$ on the unit ball of $L^2(0,1)$, $w$ uniform.
The Lean files are \texttt{SpinGlasses/AsymptoticGibbsMeasure.lean} and the general inputs \texttt{ForMathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSectionMeasurable.lean} and \texttt{ForMathlib/MeasureTheory/Constructions/BorelSpace/MeasurableOfDist.lean}.
Mathlib puts no $\sigma$-algebra on $L^2(0,1)$; the two theorems below are stated for an arbitrary Borel structure on it, given as instance arguments (any two Borel structures are equal, so this loses nothing). The measurability lemma is stated for general $L^p(\nu,E)$ because the proof does not use $p=2$.

\begin{lemma}[The $L^2$ class of a jointly measurable family is measurable]\label{lem:sg:lp-section}
  \lean{measurable_of_measurable_dist,
        MeasureTheory.inner_toLp_two,
        MeasureTheory.norm_sq_toLp_two,
        MeasureTheory.measurable_toLp_section}
  \leanok
Let $(B,\nu)$ be an s-finite measure space, $E$ a second countable normed group with its Borel $\sigma$-algebra, $1\le p<\infty$ and $L^p(\nu,E)$ separable. Let $A$ be a measurable space and $F:A\times B\to E$ measurable with $F(a,\cdot)\in L^p(\nu,E)$ for every $a$. Then $a\mapsto[F(a,\cdot)]\in L^p(\nu,E)$ is measurable for the Borel $\sigma$-algebra of $L^p(\nu,E)$. Moreover, for $p=2$ and real values, $\langle[f],[g]\rangle=\int fg\,d\nu$ and $\|[f]\|^2=\int f^2\,d\nu$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
A map into a separable metric space is measurable as soon as its distance to every point $d$ is measurable (open sets are countable unions of balls centred in a countable dense set). Here $\|[F(a,\cdot)]-d\|=\big(\int\|F(a,b)-d(b)\|^p\,d\nu(b)\big)^{1/p}$ is measurable in $a$ by Fubini, since $(a,b)\mapsto\|F(a,b)-d(b)\|^p$ is measurable. The formulas for $p=2$ are the integral formula for the inner product of $L^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A random measure on $L^2(0,1)$ from the representation]\label{thm:sg:gibbs-representation}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_gibbs_measure_of_representation}
  \leanok
Let $h:[0,1]^3\to\mathbb R$ be measurable with $\int_0^1h(w,u,v)^2\,dv\le1$ for all $(w,u)$. There is a map $G$ from $[0,1]$ to the probability measures on $L^2(0,1)$ which is measurable for the Giry $\sigma$-algebra, with $G(w)(\{\|x\|\le1\})=1$ for all $w$, such that for every bounded continuous $g$ of an array
\[\int g\Big(\big(\mathbf 1_{l=l'}+\mathbf 1_{l\ne l'}\textstyle\int h(w,u_l,v)h(w,u_{l'},v)\,dv\big)_{l,l'}\Big)\,dw\,du=\int\!\!\int g\Big(\big(\mathbf 1_{l=l'}+\mathbf 1_{l\ne l'}\langle x_l,x_{l'}\rangle\big)_{l,l'}\Big)\,dG(w)^{\otimes\mathbb N}(x)\,dw,\]
where on the left $w$ and $(u_l)_l$ are i.i.d.\ uniform on $[0,1]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{lem:sg:lp-section}
Put $\Phi(w,u)=[h(w,u,\cdot)]\in L^2(0,1)$, measurable by Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lp-section}, with $\|\Phi(w,u)\|^2=\int h(w,u,v)^2dv\le1$, and $G(w)=\Phi(w,\cdot)_*\mathrm{Leb}$; this is a probability measure on the unit ball, and $w\mapsto G(w)$ is measurable because $\Phi$ is jointly measurable (the law of a section depends measurably on the parameter). The $\mathbb N$-fold product $G(w)^{\otimes\mathbb N}$ is the image of $\mathrm{Leb}^{\otimes\mathbb N}$ under $(u_l)\mapsto(\Phi(w,u_l))_l$. Since $\langle\Phi(w,u),\Phi(w,u')\rangle=\int h(w,u,v)h(w,u',v)\,dv$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:sg:lp-section}), the array on the left is the Gram array of $(\Phi(w,u_l))_l$, and Fubini over $w$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The asymptotic Gibbs measure of the SK model]\label{thm:sg:asymptotic-gibbs-measure}
  \lean{SpinGlasses.exists_sk_asymptotic_gibbs_measure}
  \leanok
Let $H_N(\sigma)=N^{-1/2}\sum_{i,j}g_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j$ with $(g_{ij})$ i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian, $R_N(\sigma,\tau)=N^{-1}\sum_i\sigma_i\tau_i$, and let $\Lambda_N$ be the law of $(R_N(\sigma^l,\sigma^{l'}))_{l,l'}$ when the replicas $\sigma^l$ are i.i.d.\ from the Gibbs measure $G_N\propto e^{\beta H_N}$ given the disorder (averaged over the disorder). There are a strictly increasing $\varphi:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$ and a measurable map $G$ from $[0,1]$ to the probability measures on $L^2(0,1)$, each carried by the closed unit ball, such that for every bounded continuous function $g$ of an array
\[\int g\,d\Lambda_{\varphi(n)+1}\ \longrightarrow\ \int_0^1\!\!\int g\Big(\big(\mathbf 1_{l=l'}+\mathbf 1_{l\ne l'}\langle x_l,x_{l'}\rangle\big)_{l,l'}\Big)\,dG(w)^{\otimes\mathbb N}(x)\,dw\qquad(n\to\infty).\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \leanok
\uses{thm:sg:asymptotic-overlap, thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov-gram, thm:sg:gibbs-representation}
Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:asymptotic-overlap} gives a subsequence along which $\Lambda_{N+1}$ converges weakly to an exchangeable law $\mu$ on Gram-type arrays. By Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:dovbysh-sudakov-gram}, $\mu$ is the law of the array with entries $1$ on the diagonal and $\int h(w,u_l,v)h(w,u_{l'},v)\,dv$ off it, with $\int h^2\le1$. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:gibbs-representation} to this $h$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Differences from the book and what is left]\label{rem:sg:gibbs-measure-deviation}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The diagonal.} The overlap of a configuration with itself is $1$, so the limit array has $1$ on the diagonal; this is not $\langle x_l,x_l\rangle=\|x_l\|^2\le1$. The Gram array in the statement is therefore $\mathbf 1_{l=l'}+\mathbf 1_{l\ne l'}\langle x_l,x_{l'}\rangle$. In the book the discrepancy $1-\|x_l\|^2$ is the term $a_l$ of the representation, and is absorbed in the notation $(h_l\cdot h_{l'}+a_l\delta_{l,l'})$.
\item \emph{No Lemma~1.7.} The measure $G(w)$ is built directly from the function $h$ of the representation. Lemma~1.7 (Section~\ref{sec:sg:reconstruction}) would show that $G$ is determined by $\mu$ up to an isometry, so that it is canonical; this is not used and the uniqueness statement for $G$ is not formalized. The constructed $G$ is a particular representative.
\item \emph{Marginal on $L^2$ only.} The book's $\eta(w)$ lives on $L^2(0,1)\times[0,\infty)$ and $G(w)$ is its marginal on the Hilbert space. We construct only $G(w)$. Reason: the SK application uses the off-diagonal Gram array, which depends on $G$ alone, and the pair $(h_l,a_l)$ has $a_l=1-\|h_l\|^2$ determined by $h_l$ when the diagonal is $1$.
\item \emph{A Borel structure as a hypothesis.} Since Mathlib has no $\sigma$-algebra on $L^2$, it is an instance argument of the two theorems. Any two Borel structures coincide, so the theorems hold for the canonical one.
\item \emph{The Gram array is written with an explicit diagonal.} The book writes $(h_l\cdot h_{l'})_{l\ne l'}$; the Lean array is indexed by all $(l,l')$ because the limit law of Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:asymptotic-overlap} is a law on arrays with diagonal $1$.
\item \emph{Left to do.} The limit $a_N\to1$ in the Gram approximation (Section~\ref{sec:sg:aN-limit}) and the Chapter~3 uses of Theorem~1.3 (random Ruelle cascades; the countable-atom statement itself is Theorem~\ref{thm:sg:gram13-countable}). The book does not apply Theorem~1.3 to $G$ itself; it applies it to the finite measures $G^\pi_N$ and to Ruelle cascades in Chapter~3 (see Section~\ref{sec:sg:gram-background}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}
